Powers of Ghost ideals Thanks: S.E. and S.O. were supported by Grant PID2020-113206GB-I00 funded by MICIU/AEI/10.13039/501100011033 and by Grant 22004/PI/22 funded by Fundación Séneca- Agencia de Ciencia y Tecnología de la Región de Murcia. X.H.F. was partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China, Grant No. 12071064. I.H. was partially supported by NSF Grant DMS 12-01523.
S. Estrada, X.H. Fu, I. Herzog, S. Odabaşı

Abstract
. Let ( 𝒜 ; ℰ ) (\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E}) be an exact category and 𝒮 \mathcal{S} a set of objects in 𝒜 . \mathcal{A}. A morphism f : X → Y f:X\to Y is called an 𝒮 \mathcal{S} -ghost map if Ext ⁡ ( S , f ) = 0 {\rm Ext}(S,f)=0 for each S ∈ 𝒮 . S\in\mathcal{S}. A theory of ordinal powers of the ideal 𝔤 𝒮 \mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}} of 𝒮 \mathcal{S} -ghost morphisms is developed by introducing for every ordinal λ \lambda , the λ \lambda -th inductive power 𝒥 ( λ ) \mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)} of an ideal 𝒥 . \mathcal{J}. The Generalized λ \lambda -Generating Hypothesis ( λ \lambda -GGH) for an ideal 𝒥 \mathcal{J} of an exact category 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is the proposition that the λ \lambda -th inductive power 𝒥 ( λ ) {\mathcal{J}}^{(\lambda)} is an object ideal. It is shown that under mild conditions every inductive power of a ghost ideal is an object-special preenveloping ideal. When λ \lambda is infinite, the proof is based on an ideal version of Eklof’s Lemma, which states that if an object C ∈ 𝒜 C\in\mathcal{A} admits a λ \lambda - ⟂ 𝒥 {}^{\perp}\mathcal{J} -filtration, then C C is left orthogonal to 𝒥 ( λ ) . \mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}. When λ \lambda is an infinite regular cardinal, the Generalized λ \lambda -Generating Hypothesis is established for the ghost ideal 𝔤 𝒮 \mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}} for the case when 𝒜 \mathcal{A} a locally λ \lambda -presentable Grothendieck category and 𝒮 \mathcal{S} is a set of λ \lambda -presentable objects in 𝒜 \mathcal{A} such that ( 𝒮 ⟂ ) ⟂ {}^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}) contains a generating set for 𝒜 . \mathcal{A}. As a consequence of ω \omega -GGH for the ghost ideal 𝔤 R ​ -mod \mathfrak{g}_{R{\rm\mbox{-mod}}} in the category of modules R ​ - ​ Mod R\mbox{-}{\rm Mod} over a ring, it is shown that if the class of pure projective left R R -modules is closed under extensions, then every left FP-projective module is pure projective. A restricted version n n -GGH( 𝔤 ⁡ ( 𝐂 ⁡ ( R ) ) \mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)) ) for the ghost ideal in OPEN 𝐂 ⁡ ( R ) ) \mathbf{C}(R)) is also considered and it is shown that n n -GGH( 𝔤 ⁡ ( 𝐂 ⁡ ( R ) ) \mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)) ) holds for R R if and only if the n n -th power of the ghost ideal in the derived category 𝐃 ⁡ ( R ) \mathbf{D}(R) is zero if and only if the global dimension of R R is less than n . n. If R R is coherent, then the Generating Hypothesis holds for R R if and only if R R is von Neumann regular.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
18E10;18G25;18G35;16D90

1.
Introduction

In algebraic topology and its categorical counterpart, the theory of triangulated categories, a
ghost
is a general term for a morphism that belongs to the kernel of some particular functor. For example, a map
f\colon X\to Y
in the homotopy category of spectra is called a ghost if the induced maps of homotopy groups
\pi(f):\pi(X)\to\pi(Y)
are zero. It is the subject of a longstanding conjecture.

Freyd’s Generating Hypothesis (GH).

[
16
, §9]

Every ghost map between finite spectra is trivial.

The class of ghost maps in the homotopy category of (finite) spectra forms an ideal so the GH can be restated to say that the ghost ideal in the homotopy category of finite spectra is zero.

Kelly
[
29
]
explored the possibility of when finitely many ghosts compose to
0
in the homotopy category
\mathbf{K}(\mathcal{A}),
where
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category with enough projectives. Working, as we will be here, in the exact category
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
of chain complexes, this means that the finite composition of ghosts belongs to the “object” ideal of morphisms that factor though a null-homotopic chain complex. Christensen
[
11
]
makes explicit reference to the term
ghost
in the derived category
\mathbf{D}(\mathcal{A})
, which is defined to be a chain map
f:X\to Y
whose induced maps on homology
\operatorname{H}_{i}(f):\operatorname{H}_{i}(X)\to\operatorname{H}_{i}(Y)
are zero. In the work of Beligiannis
[
4
, Section 1]
, a morphism in an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is called a
T
-ghost, for an object
T\in\mathcal{A},
if it is invisible to the representable functor
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(T,-).

Lockridge
[
30
]
initiated the study of GH in a triangulated category and proved that it holds in the derived category
\mathbf{D}^{c}(R),
where
R
is commutative, if and only if
R
is von Neumann regular. For a ring
R,
the GH states that the ghost ideal in the subcategory
\mathbf{D}^{c}(R)
of perfect complexes is zero. Hovey, Lockridge and Puninski
[
27
]
generalized Lockridge’s result to the noncommutative case.

At around the same time, Benson, Chebolu, Christensen and Mináč
[
5
]
formulated the GH in the stable module category
kG
-
\underline{\rm mod}
of a finite
p
-group. Here a ghost is a map between
kG
-modules that is trivial in Tate cohomology, and GH states that the ghost ideal in the stable category
kG
-
\underline{\rm mod}
of finitely generated
kG
-modules is zero. They show that GH holds for a non-trivial finite
p
-group if and only if
G
is
C_{2}
or
C_{3}.
If
G
is a
p
-group not equal to
C_{2}
or
C_{3},
then Chebolu, Christensen and Mináč
[
9
]
showed that the ghost ideal in
kG
-
\underline{\rm mod}
is nilpotent with index of nilpotency less than
|G|.
The same authors
[
10
]
also considered the
Generalized Generating Hypothesis (GGH),
which states that the ghost ideal is zero in the ambient stable category
kG
-
\underline{\rm Mod}
of all
kG
-modules. They showed that it holds if and only if the group is
C_{2}
or
C_{3}.

Hovey, Lockridge and Puninski suggest the following ring-theoretic proposition as a generalization of GH for a positive integer
n.

The
n
-Fold Generating Hypothesis (
n
-GH).

[
27
, p. 799]

The
n
-th power of the ghost ideal in
\mathbf{D}^{c}(R)
is zero.

Hovey and Lockridge
[
25
]
proved that
n
-GH holds for a ring if and only if the weak global dimension is less than
n.
Regarding the ghost ideal in the ambient derived category
\mathbf{D}(R),
we can combine
[
11
, Corollary 8.3]
with
[
26
]
to obtain that the
n
-GGH holds (see below, the generalization of
n
-GH to the ambient category) for a ring if and only if the global dimension of
R
is less than
n.
This suggests that one may use the nilpotency index of the ghost ideal in
\mathbf{D}(R)
to recover the global dimension.

In this paper, we will work in an exact category
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E}).
If
{\mathcal{S}}
is a set of objects in
\mathcal{A},
then the
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost ideal

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
(Definition
3.2
) consists of morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
satisfying
{\rm Ext}(S,f)=0
for any
S\in{\mathcal{S}}.
This is a unifying notion that includes both the ghosts of Kelly in the homotopy category (Example
3.4
) as well as those of Benson, Chebolu, Christensen and Mináč in the stable category (Example
3.3
).

Inspired by previous work on the (Generalized) GH in the derived category and in modular representation theory we formulate
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
) in an exact category as follows. The notation
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
stands for the
\lambda
-th inductive power of an ideal
\mathcal{J}
(see §5.2), and if
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is additive, then
\langle\mathcal{C}\rangle
is the ideal of morphisms that factor through some object in
\mathcal{C}.

The Generalized
\lambda
-Generating Hypothesis (
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
))

Let
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
be an exact category,
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
a subset and
\lambda
an ordinal. Then

\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}=\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle,

where
{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}=\{X\in\mathcal{A}\,|\,{\rm Ext}(S,X)=0\}.

The main result of the paper may then be stated as follows.

Theorem
7.5
.

Let
\lambda
be an infinite regular cardinal and
\mathcal{A}
a locally
\lambda
-presentable Grothendieck category. If
\mathcal{S}
is a set of
\lambda
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{A}
such that
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp})
contains a generating set for
\mathcal{A}
, then
\lambda
-
GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
holds.

Essential to the statement of
\lambda
-GGH, and instrumental in the proof of Theorem
7.5
, is the definition of the inductive ordinal power
\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}
of an ideal
\mathcal{J};
it is defined as the ideal generated by compositions of postcomposable
\alpha
-sequences of morphisms from
\mathcal{J}
(see §5.2). These powers form a descending filtration

(1)

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}^{(1)}\supseteq\mathcal{J}^{(2)}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}\supseteq\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha+1)}\supseteq\cdots

of ideals, bounded below by the object ideal
\langle{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{J})\rangle.
The Generalized
\lambda
-Generating Hypothesis for a general ideal
\mathcal{J}
is the convergence statement
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathcal{J}
):
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}=\langle{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{J})\rangle.

The filtration (
1
) resembles, for example, the descending filtration of powers of the Jacobson radical in the category
\Lambda
-
{\rm mod}
of finitely presented modules over an artin algebra, but in contrast to previously defined filtrations of ordinal powers of ideals (see, for example,
[
40
,
33
,
34
]
), the definition at a limit ordinal
\lambda
is
not
given by
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}:=\bigcap_{\alpha<\lambda}\,\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}.

We will apply an ideal version of the Eklof Lemma to show (Theorem
6.2
) that the ordinal inductive powers
\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}
inherit from
\mathcal{J}
the property of being object-special preenveloping: if
\mathcal{J}
is object-special preenveloping, then so are all the ideals of the filtration (
1
). Recall that an ideal
\mathcal{J}
is
object-special preenveloping
if for every object
A\in\mathcal{A},
there exists an inflation

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

with
j\in\mathcal{J}
and
C\in{\rm Ob}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}).
In that case, the object
C\in{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}
is called a
\mathcal{J}
-cosyzygy of
A
and a subcategory
\Omega^{\mbox{\rm-}1}(\mathcal{J})\subseteq{\rm Ob}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J})
is a
\mathcal{J}
-
cosysygy category
if it contains a
\mathcal{J}
-cosyzygy for every object
A
of the category. A crucial property of object-special preenveloping ideals is that the orthogonal ideal
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}
may be characterized
[
18
, Proposition 7.3]
as the least left perpendicular object ideal containing some (resp., any)
\mathcal{J}
-cosyzygy category
\Omega^{\mbox{\rm-}1}(\mathcal{J})
(see §3.1).

The ideal version of Eklof’s Lemma is a general statement about ideals
\mathcal{J}
that relates the objects of the left perpendicular ideal
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}
with those of
{{}^{\perp}}(\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}).

Theorem
5.13
.
(The Ideal Eklof Lemma)
Let
\mathcal{J}
be an ideal of
\mathcal{A}
and
\alpha
an ordinal. If an object
C
in
\mathcal{A}
has an
\alpha
-filtration in
\operatorname{Ob}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}),
then
C\in{{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)})}.

If we denote by
\alpha
-
\rm Filt(\mathcal{C})
the subcategory of objects that admit an
\alpha
-filtration by objects in
\mathcal{C},
then the Ideal Eklof Lemma may be stated more concisely as the inclusion

\alpha\mbox{\rm-}\rm Filt({\rm Ob}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}))\subseteq{\rm Ob}({{}^{\perp}}(\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)})).

The technical proof of the the Ideal Eklof Lemma is by induction on the ordinal
\alpha.
The successor case is treated in §4; the limit case in §5. The successor case elaborates on the adjustment technique originally used in Eklof’s proof. The
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}
-complex between two conflations in an exact category is used to find a method of downward adjustment (see §4.3) for a morphism of trivial conflations. The method identifies conditions under which the morphism, regarded as a conflation of arrows, is itself trivial. This downward adjustment method leads to a general version of the classical Eklof Lemma (Corollary
5.12
) as well as a condition (Theorem
5.9
), relevant in the limit case, for a conflation of
\lambda
-systems that is trivial at every ordinal

\alpha<\lambda
to be globally trivial. We point out that while some arguments in §5 clearly take inspiration from those of Št́ovíček
[
39
, Proposition 5.7]
our goals require slight differences in argumentation.

These ideas are brought together in §6 to prove the following (see Hypothesis
6.1
).

Theorem
6.2
.

If
\mathcal{J}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal with
\Omega^{\mbox{\rm-}1}(\mathcal{J})
a cosyzygy subcategory, then for every ordinal
\alpha,
the
\alpha
-th inductive power
\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal with cosyzygy category
\Omega^{\mbox{\rm-}1}(\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)})=\alpha\mbox{\rm-}\rm Filt(\Omega^{\mbox{\rm-}1}(\mathcal{J})).

Applying this scenario to the ghost ideal
\mathcal{J}=\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
yields a proof by induction that every
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(\alpha)}
is object-special preenveloping. The base case
\alpha=1
is given by the following. It also states that
{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}})=\Omega^{\mbox{\rm-}1}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
is a
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
-cosyzygy category; note that
{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq{{}^{\perp}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
follows from the definition of an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost ideal.

Theorem
3.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}.
If
\mathcal{A}
has exact coproducts, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal with the class
{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
as a

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
-cosyzygy subcategory. More precisely, for any object
A\in\mathcal{A},
there is a conflation

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W}

with
g
an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost map, and
W\in{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}}).

To summarize, let us sketch the proof of Theorem
7.5
. For an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost ideal
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
, there is a descending filtration of inductive powers of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}\supseteq\mathfrak{g}^{2}_{\mathcal{S}}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq\mathfrak{g}^{(\alpha)}_{\mathcal{S}}\supseteq\cdots

bounded below by
{\rm Ob}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})=\langle\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\rangle.
Using Theorem
6.2
, we may consider the ascending filtration of cosyzygy subcategories

{\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S})\subseteq 2\mbox{\rm-}\rm Filt({\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S}))\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\alpha\mbox{\rm-}\rm Filt({\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S}))\subseteq\cdots

of
{{}^{\perp}}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}).
When the category is a locally
\lambda
-presentable Grothendieck category, and
\mathcal{S}
is a set of
\lambda
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{A}
, the work of Št́ovíček
[
38
]
(and Enochs
[
12
]
for a module category) provides the explicit bound
\lambda
on the length of a filtration in
{{}^{\perp}}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})=\lambda\operatorname{-}\rm Filt({\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S})).
This implies that the
\lambda
-th inductive power
\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}=\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle
is already an object ideal and that
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
) holds. In this last step of the proof, the convergence statement
\lambda
-GGH is seen as
{\rm Ext}
-orthogonal to the statement, established by Št́ovíček and Enochs, on the convergence of the associated ascending filtration of subcategories.

The methods employed in this paper are a part of Ideal Approximation Theory, which is devoted to the study of
ideal cotorsion pairs,
that is, maximal pairs of
{\rm Ext}
-orthogonal ideals. The fundamental theorem is an ideal version of Salce’s Lemma
[
17
]
, which states that for an ideal cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
the ideal
\mathcal{I}
is special precovering if and only if
\mathcal{J}
is special preenveloping. Such ideal cotorsion pairs are called
complete
and we contribute to the theory here by proving that if
({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J},\mathcal{J})
is a complete ideal cotorsion pair, with
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}
an object ideal, then so is
({{}^{\perp}}(\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}),\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}),
for any ordinal
\alpha.
The special case when
\alpha=n
is finite follows from an ideal version of Christensen’s Lemma
[
18
, Theorem 8.4]
, which was used to settle the Benson-Gnacadja Conjecture
[
18
, Corollary 9.4]
by showing that the ideal of phantom morphisms in the stable module category
kG
-
\underline{\rm Mod}
of a finite group ring is nilpotent with index of nilpotency bounded by
|G|.
Theorem
3.6
above may be regarded as an ideal version of the Eklof-Trlifaj Lemma
[
21
, Theorem 3.2.1]
, whose main consequence is that a cotorsion pair (of subcategories rather than ideals) generated by a set
{\mathcal{S}}
is complete; the
ideal
cotorsion pair generated by
{\mathcal{S}}
is
({{}^{\perp}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}),\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}).

The main contributions of the paper to the theory are: 1) the introduction of the notion
\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}
of an inductive ordinal power of an ideal
\mathcal{J};
2) the further development of Ideal Approximation Theory by using the inductive ordinal powers of an ideal to formulate and prove an ideal variant of the classical version of Eklof’s Lemma; and finally 3) an application that establishes the connection with the Generalized Generating Hypothesis and confirms the correctness of the ideal variant. Let us describe some instances of
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
) in closer detail.

The proper setting for Ideal Approximation Theory is that of an exact category
[
8
]
. It allows us to not only consider complete ideal cotorsion pairs in categories of modules, but also in more general situations, such as the category
{\bf C}(R)
of chain complexes in
R\mbox{-}{\rm Mod}
, the category
\operatorname{dg-Proj}
of
dg
-projective chain complexes in
\mathbf{C}(R)
, etc. As in the derived category
\mathbf{D}(R)
, we call a chain map
f
in the category
{\bf C}(R)
of complexes a
ghost
if

\operatorname{H}_{i}(f)=0
for any integer
i.

It turns out that the ideal
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
of ghost maps in
{\bf C}(R)
is not only object-special preenveloping, but also object-special precovering. Indeed, it is shown in Proposition
8.8
that there exists a complete ideal cotorsion triple

(\langle\operatorname{CE-Proj}\rangle,\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)),\langle\operatorname{CE-Inj}\rangle),

where
\operatorname{CE-Proj}
(resp.,
\operatorname{CE-Inj}
) is the category of Cartan-Eilenberg projective (resp., injective) complexes.

The Enochs-Št́ovíček bound on filtrations of Cartan-Eilenberg projective complexes is given by
\omega,
so that
\omega
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
) holds and the inductive powers of the ideal
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
already converge to the object ideal
\langle\operatorname{Acyc}\rangle
at stage
\omega.
But if the global dimension of
R
is less than
n
, then it will converge at stage
n.
This is confirmed by the theory developed in this paper.

Theorem
8.14
.

Let
n\geq 0.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))^{n+1}=0
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{n+1}=\langle\operatorname{Acyc}\rangle,
i.e.,
(n+1)
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)))
holds;

(iii)

\mathfrak{g}(\operatorname{dg-Proj})^{n+1}=\langle\operatorname{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rangle
;

(iv)

\operatorname{l.gl.dim}(R)\leq n
.

That (iv
\Rightarrow
i) is just
[
11
, Corollary 8.4]
, and (i
\Rightarrow
iv) appears in
[
26
]
. Applying the same techniques for the ghost ideal between finitely presented chain complexes over a coherent ring, we also obtain the following result, in which the equivalence (i
\Leftrightarrow
iv) is just
[
27
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Theorem
8.18
.

Suppose that
R
is left coherent. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}^{c}(R))=0
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}^{\operatorname{fp}}(R))=\langle\operatorname{acyc}^{b}\rangle
;

(iii)

\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}^{b}(\operatorname{proj}))=\langle\operatorname{proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rangle
;
and

(iv)

R

is von Neumann regular,

where
\operatorname{proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))
denotes the class of finitely generated projective chain complexes.

The most important example of a cotorsion pair in the category of
R
-modules may very well be the pair
(R\mbox{-}{\rm Flat},R\mbox{-}{\rm Cotor})
cogenerated by the subcategory
R\mbox{-}{\rm PInj}\subseteq R\mbox{-}{\rm Cotor}
of pure injective modules. The completeness property of this pair, better known as The Flat Cover Conjecture, was established by Bican, El Bashir and Enochs
[
6
]
. The important special case of a ring
R
over which every left cotorsion module is pure injective, i.e.
R\mbox{-}{\rm Cotor}=R\mbox{-}{\rm PInj},
was considered by Xu
[
41
]
in 1996, who proved that this condition is equivalent to the class of pure injective modules being closed under extension. His proof, which relies on the existence of pure injective envelopes, follows readily from Wakamatsu’s Lemma. The absence of pure projective covers, however, does not allow us to dualize Xu’s proof, but it still makes sense to conjecture the dual of Xu’s result for the cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{FP},\mathcal{FI})
generated by the subcategory
R\mbox{-}{\rm PProj}
of pure projective left
R
-modules, where
\mathcal{FP}
is the subcategory of FP-projective modules, and
\mathcal{FI}
is the subcategory of FP-injective modules. In
[
18
, Proposition 10.8]
, the second and third authors reprove Xu’s result using the ideal approximation theory developed in that paper, but that proof doesn’t permit dualization either. In the last sentence of
[
18
]
, the second and third authors promised that they will develop a theory to prove a special case of the dual. We present that general case in this paper!

Theorem
9.7
.

Let
R
be an associative ring. Every left FP-projective
R
-module is pure projective if and only if
R\mbox{-}{\rm PProj}\subseteq R\mbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is closed under extensions.

The proof of this result starts from an analysis of the notion of
FP-ghost
ideal
\Psi
introduced in Section 9, and heavily relies on Theorem
7.5
, which reveals the importance of The Generalized Generating Hypothesis. For artin algebras, we have the following two sufficient conditions for
n
-GGH(
\Psi
) to hold.

Corollary
9.5

Let
R
be an artin algebra, and
n
a positive integer. Then
n
-
GGH(
\Psi
)
holds provided one of the following two conditions is satisfied.

(i)

R
has global dimension less than
n;

(ii)

R
has pure global dimension less than
n.

Proposition
9.6
.

If
R
is a left artinian ring with
J^{n}=0
then
n
-
GGH(
\Psi
)
holds.

The paper ends with some open problems related to the contents of this work.

2.
Preliminaries

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category. An
ideal

\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{A}
is an additive subfunctor of
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}:{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm{\footnotesize op}}}\times\mathcal{A}\to{\rm{\bf\mbox{Ab}}}
, or equivalently, for every morphisms
f:A\to X
,
h:Y\to B
in
\mathcal{A}
and
g:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{I}(X,Y)
, the composition

hgf:A\to B
belongs to
\mathcal{I}(A,B)
; and for any two morphisms
g_{1}:X\to Y
and
g_{2}:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{I}(X,Y)
,
g_{1}-g_{2}
also belongs to
\mathcal{I}(X,Y)
.

For given ideals
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{A}
,
the product ideal

\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J}
is the smallest ideal containing all compositions
ij
with
i\in\mathcal{I}
and
j\in\mathcal{J}
composable. One can easily verify that the product ideal
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J}
in fact consists of all those compositions

\mathcal{I}\mathcal{J}=\{ij\mid\ {}i\in\mathcal{I},{}j\in\mathcal{J}\},

whenever the pair
(i,j)
is composable.

For a given ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{A}
, an object
A\in\mathcal{A}
is said to belong to
\mathcal{I},
denoted by
A\in\mathcal{I},
if
1_{A}\in\mathcal{I}(A,A).
We let
{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{I})
be the subcategory consisting of objects in
\mathcal{I}
. By
[
18
, Proposition 2.1]
,
{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{I})
is an additive subategory of
\mathcal{A}
, that is,
{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{I})
is closed under finite direct sums and direct summands.
We let
\langle{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{I})\rangle
denote the collection of morphims factoring through an object in
{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{I})
, which is clearly an ideal.

\mathcal{I}
is said to be an
object ideal
if
\mathcal{I}=\langle{\rm Ob}(\mathcal{I})\rangle
.

Throughout this paper, we will work in the context of an exact category. As the reference of exact categories, we heavily rely on
[
8
]
, but we use the terminology from Keller
[
28
]
. An exact category
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
is an additive category
\mathcal{A}
equipped with an exact structure
\mathcal{E}
, where the
exact structure
\mathcal{E}
is an isomorphism-closed collection of distinguished kernel-cokernel pairs
(i,p)
in
\mathcal{A}

\Xi:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.48206pt\raise 5.31596pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.31596pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.71954pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
i
is called the
inflation
and
p
the
deflation
of
\Xi
, respectively,
satisfying:

(i)

For every object
A\in\mathcal{A},
the identity morphism
1_{A}
is both an inflation and a deflation;

(ii)

Inflations and deflations are closed under composition;

(iii)

The class
\mathcal{E}
is closed under pushout and pullback along any morphism in
\mathcal{A}
.

A kernel-cokernel pair
(i,p)
, or
\Xi
, in
\mathcal{E}
is called a
conflation in

(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
. We will also sometimes say that
C
is the cokernel of
i
and use the notation
C=B/A;

A
is the kernel of
p,
which may be denoted by
A\subseteq B
. For given objects
A
and
C
in
\mathcal{A}
, we will assume that the isomorphism classes of conflations
\Xi
in
\mathcal{E}
form a set denoted by
{\rm Ext}(C,A)
, and therefore, it is an additive group with respect to the
Baer sum
operation. Hence, a conflation
\Xi:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.48206pt\raise 5.31596pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.31596pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.71954pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
in
\mathcal{E}
represents
0
in
{\rm Ext}(C,A)
if it is
trivial
, that is, the morphism
i
is a section: there exists a retraction
r:B\to A
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
ri=1_{A}
; or equivalently,
p
is a retraction: there exists a morphism
s:C\rightarrow B
such that
ps=1_{C}
; see
[
8
, Remark 7.4]
.

From now on, the category
\mathcal{A}
is assumed to be equipped with an exact structure
\mathcal{E}
unless otherwise is stated. Note that the rule
(C,A)\mapsto{\rm Ext}(C,A)
is an additive bifunctor from
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{op}}\times\mathcal{A}
to
Ab
. For a morphism
f:A\to X
and an object
C
in
\mathcal{A}
, the morphism
{\rm Ext}(C,f):{\rm Ext}(C,A)\to{\rm Ext}(C,X)
sends a conflation
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{C}
in
{\rm Ext}(C,A)
to the conflation
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}
in
{\rm Ext}(X,C)
appearing in the following pushout diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C.}

Therefore,
{\rm Ext}(C,f)=0
if and only if for any conflation
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{C}
in
{\rm Ext}(C,A)
, the conflation
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}
in the bottom row is trivial.

For a morphism
g:Y\to C
and an object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
, the morphism
{\rm Ext}(g,A):{\rm Ext}(C,A)\to{\rm Ext}(Y,A)
is defined dually.
We have
{\rm Ext}(f,g)={\rm Ext}(f,A){\rm Ext}(g,Y)={\rm Ext}(g,C){\rm Ext}(f,X):{\rm Ext}(C,A)\to{\rm Ext}(Y,X).

The following lemma not only describes the finite version
(\lambda=n)
of Theorem
5.13
, but also illustrates the argument behind the inductive step of the transfinite case.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
f=f_{n}f_{n-1}\cdots f_{1}
be a sequence of composable morphisms in
\mathcal{A}

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.77777pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.77777pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.48431pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.77777pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.77777pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.52597pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.33331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.33331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 114.83331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 114.83331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 144.56245pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.02664pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.02664pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{n}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

If
0=C_{0}\subseteq C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq C_{n}=C
is a sequence of inflations in
\mathcal{A}
such that for each
1\leq i\leq n
,

{\rm Ext}(C_{i}/C_{i-1},f_{i})=0
, then
{\rm Ext}(C,f)=0
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction. So we only need to prove this result for
n=2
. Applying the functor
{\rm Ext}(-,f)={\rm Ext}(-,f_{2}){\rm Ext}(-,f_{1})
to the conflation
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.1743pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.1743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.69455pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.1743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.1743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.18475pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.5229pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 69.5229pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{2}/C_{1}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,
we obtain a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2}/C_{1},X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2},X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{1})}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{1},X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{1},f_{1})=0}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2}/C_{1},X_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(\pi,X_{2})}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2}/C_{1},f_{2})=0}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2},X_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(\iota,X_{2})}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{2})}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{1},X_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2}/C_{1},X_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{2},X_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(C_{1},X_{3})}

with exact rows. Let
a\in{\rm Ext}(C_{2},X_{1})
. Since
{\rm Ext}(C_{1},f_{1})=0
, then from the right upper commutative square, we have that
{\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{1})(a)
belongs to the kernel of
{\rm Ext}(\iota,X_{2})
, and hence belongs to the image of
{\rm Ext}(\pi,X_{2})
. Let
b\in{\rm Ext}(C_{2}/C_{1},X_{2})
such that
{\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{1})(a)={\rm Ext}(\pi,X_{2})(b)
. Then from the lower left commutative square, we obtain that
{\rm Ext}(C_{2},f)(a)={\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{2}){\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{1})(a)={\rm Ext}(C_{2},f_{2}){\rm Ext}(\pi,X_{2})(b)={\rm Ext}(\pi,X_{3}){\rm Ext}(C_{2}/C_{1},f_{2})(b)=0
, as desired.
∎

For a given class
\mathcal{S}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, we let

\mathcal{S}^{\perp}=\{A\in\mathcal{A}\mid{\rm Ext}(S,A)=0,\textrm{ for every }S\in\mathcal{S}\}
and by
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}=\{A\in\mathcal{A}\mid{\rm Ext}(A,S)=0,\textrm{ for every }S\in\mathcal{S}\}.
Then a pair
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C})
of classes of objects in
\mathcal{A}
is called a
cotorsion pair
if
\mathcal{F}^{\perp}=\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}={}^{\perp}\mathcal{C}
. A cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C})
is said to
have enough injectives
if for every object
A
of
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a conflation in
\mathcal{A}
of the form

A\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow F

with
C\in\mathcal{C}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
. That a cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C})
is said to be
have enough projectives
is defined dually.
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{C})
is a

complete cotorsion pair
if it has both enough projectives and enough injectives. Similarly, for a given class
\mathcal{M}
of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
, we let
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{M}
denote the classes of morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
{\rm Ext}(m,f)=0
and
{\rm Ext}(f,m)=0
for every
m
in
\mathcal{M}
, respectively. It is clear that both classes
\mathcal{M}^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{M}
are ideals in
\mathcal{A}
. A pair
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
of ideals in
\mathcal{A}
is said to be an
ideal cotorsion pair
if
\mathcal{I}^{\perp}=\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{I}={}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}
.

A special case of the foregoing is the cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{E}\mbox{-Proj},\mathcal{A})
where
\mathcal{E}\mbox{-Proj}
denotes the subcategory of
projective objects
of
\mathcal{A}.
Thus an object
P\in\mathcal{A}
is projective if
{\rm Ext}(P,X)=0
for every object
X\in\mathcal{A},
or equivalently, if the functor
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(P,-)
is exact. The exact category
\mathcal{A}
is said to have
enough projectives
if the cotorsion pair
(\mathcal{E}\mbox{-Proj},\mathcal{A})
does. The notion of an
injective object
and the subcategory
\mathcal{E}\mbox{-Inj}
are defined dually.

Recall that an
exact substructure
of
\mathcal{E}
is an exact structure
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
. For a given class
{\mathcal{S}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, define
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
to be the class of all conflations
\Xi
for which the functor
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,-)
preserves the exactness of
\Xi
for every
S\in\mathcal{S}
. It is readily verified that
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is an exact substructure of
\mathcal{E}
with
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\mbox{-Proj}.
This exact substructure
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is called
the exact structure projectively generated by

{\mathcal{S}}.
The notion of
the exact structure injectively cogenerated by a class
{\mathcal{S}}
is defined dually.

Let
\mbox{Sum}(\mathcal{S})
denote the class of coproducts of objects in
\mathcal{S}
and denote by
{\rm Add}({\mathcal{S}})
(
{\rm add}({\mathcal{S}})
) the smallest subcategory containing
{\mathcal{S}}
which is closed under (finite) coproduct and direct summands. An object belongs to
\mbox{Add}({\mathcal{S}})
(
{\rm add}({\mathcal{S}})
) if and only if it is a direct summand of a (finite) coproduct of objects in
{\mathcal{S}}.
Indeed, by
[
8
, Corollary 11.4 and 11.7]
, we have that
{\rm Add}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\mbox{-Proj}.

A
generating set
for an exact category
\mathcal{A}
is a set
{\mathcal{S}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
such that every object
A\in\mathcal{A}
admits a deflation
\coprod_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\;S\to A
in
\mathcal{E}
from some coproduct of objects in
{\mathcal{S}}.
The following idea originates in the work of Stenström
[
36
, Proposition 2.3]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

If
\mathcal{A}
has coproducts, and
{\mathcal{S}}
is a generating set for
\mathcal{A}
, then the exact substructure
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
has enough projectives. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\textrm{-}{\rm Proj}={\rm Add}({\mathcal{S}}).

Proof.

Let
A
be an object in
\mathcal{A}
. By hypothesis, we have a deflation
\coprod_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\;S\to A
in
\mathcal{E}
from a coproduct of objects in
S.
The domain
\coprod_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\;S
is
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-projective. If every map from an object in
{\mathcal{S}}
to
A
is represented in this deflation, then it is an
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-deflation. If not, supplement the coproduct by adding factors to the domain so that every map from an object in
{\mathcal{S}}
to
A
is represented. By the argument dual to the equivalence (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii) of
[
8
, Proposition 2.12]
, this map is still a deflation in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{S}}
and the domain is still
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-projective.
∎

3.
Ghost ideals in an exact category

In this section, we introduce the notion of an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost ideal in
\mathcal{A}
, and study its approximation properties. We start by
showing that a finite power of an object-special preenveloping ideal is still object-special preenveloping.

3.1.
Object-special preenveloping ideals

An ideal
\mathcal{J}
in
\mathcal{A}
is said to be an
object-special preenveloping
ideal if for any object
A
, there is a conflation

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W}

with
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and
W\in{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}}
. The object
W\in{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}}
is called a
\mathcal{J}
-cosyzygy object of
A
, and is denoted by
\Omega^{-1}_{\mathcal{J}}(A)
. It is easy to check that the morphism
j
is a
\mathcal{J}
-preenvelope of
A
, that is, any morphism
j^{\prime}:A\to J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
factors through
j,
as in

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J^{\prime}.}

A subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
that is closed under finite direct sums is called a
\mathcal{J}
-
cosyzygy subcategory
if it contains a
\mathcal{J}
-cosyzygy
\Omega^{-1}_{\mathcal{J}}(A)
for every object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
. Such a subcategory will be denoted by
\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}).

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a subcategory and
n
a positive integer. An object
C
of
\mathcal{A}
is said to admit an
n
-
\mathcal{C}
-filtration
if there is a sequence of inflations

C_{0}=0\subseteq C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq C_{n-1}\subseteq C_{n}=C
with each
C_{i}/C_{i-1}\in\mathcal{C}
. We denote by
n
-
\rm Filt(\mathcal{C})
the class of objects in
\mathcal{A}
possessing an
n
-
\mathcal{C}
-filtration. The following result follows from
[
18
, Proposition 6.2 and Corollary 8.2]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be an object-special preenveloping ideal in
\mathcal{A}
with
\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J})
a cosyzygy subcategory. Then the
n
-th power
\mathcal{J}^{n}
is still an object-special preenveloping ideal with
n
-
\rm Filt(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))
a cosyzygy category.

Proof.

An object-special
\mathcal{J}^{n}
-preevelope of
A\in\mathcal{A}
can be constructed in the following way. Firstly, there is a conflation

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W}

with
j
a special
\mathcal{J}
-preenvelope of
A
, and
W=J/A\in\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J})
. For
J_{1}:=J
, there is a conflation

\textstyle{J_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{1}_{2}}
\textstyle{J_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{1}_{2}}

with
j^{1}_{2}
a special
\mathcal{J}
-preenvelope of
J_{1}
, and
W^{1}_{2}\in\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J})
. Continuing this process, there is a conflation

\textstyle{J_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{n-1}_{n}}
\textstyle{J_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{n-1}_{n}}

with
j^{n-1}_{n}
a special
\mathcal{J}
-preenvelope of
J_{n-1}
, and
W^{n-1}_{n}\in\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J})
. Composing the morphisms
j
,
j^{1}_{2}
,
\cdots,j^{n-1}_{n}
, we attain the following commutative diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 72.70026pt\raise-88.41663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern In the conflation
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{n}}
\textstyle{J_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{n}}
on the bottom,
j_{n}\in\mathcal{J}^{n}
, and
W_{n}\in n
-
\mbox{Filt}(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))
since
0\to W\to W_{2}\to\cdots\to W_{n}
is an
n
-
\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J})
-filtration. By Lemma
2.1
,
n
-
\mbox{Filt}(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))\subseteq{}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}^{n}
. Therefore
j_{n}
is an object-special
\mathcal{J}^{n}
-preenvelope of
A
.
∎

3.2.
Ghosts

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. A morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{A}
is called an
\mathcal{S}
-
ghost
if
{\rm Ext}(S,f)=0
for any
S\in\mathcal{S}
.

The
\mathcal{S}
-ghost morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
form an ideal, we call it the
\mathcal{S}
-
ghost
ideal, and denote it by
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
. It is clear that
({{}^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}},\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
is an ideal cotorsion pair and that every object in
{\rm Add}(\mathcal{S})
belongs to
{{}^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}}.
Also
{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}={\rm Ob}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
so that
\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}.

Example 3.3
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
k
a field with
{\rm char}(k)\!\mid\!o(G).
A morphism
f:M\rightarrow N
of left
kG
-modules is called a
ghost
if it induces zero on Tate cohomology, that is,
\widehat{\operatorname{H}}^{n}(G,f)=0
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, see
[
5
,
10
]
.

Denote by
P_{*}
a complete projective resolution of the trivial left
kG
-module
k
, that is, an acyclic chain complex of projective left
kG
-modules, with group of
0
-cycles
\operatorname{Z}_{0}(P_{*})\cong k,
that remains exact under the functor
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(-,M)
for every projective left
kG
-module
M.
Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the set
\{\Omega^{n}(k)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\Omega^{n}(k):=\operatorname{Z}_{n}(P_{*})
is the group of
n
-cycles of
P_{*}.
Then a morphism
f:M\rightarrow N
in
kG
-
Mod
is a ghost in the sense of
[
10
]
if and only if it is an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost.
Indeed, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\widehat{\operatorname{H}}^{n}(G,-)\cong\operatorname{H}_{-(n+1)}({\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(P_{*},-))\cong{\rm Ext}(\Omega^{n-1}(k),-);

for further details, see
[
7
, Section VI-4]
.

Example 3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{C}(R)
and
\mathbf{K}(R)
denote the category of chain complexes of left
R
-modules and the homotopy category of
\mathbf{C}(R)
, respectively.
Consider the homology functor

\operatorname{H}_{*}:\mathbf{C}(R)\longrightarrow\mathbf{K}(R)\longrightarrow R\operatorname{-Mod.}

Kelly in
[
29
]
studied morphisms in
\mathbf{K}(R)
that are invisible under the homology functor, so we call a morphism
f
in
\mathbf{C}(R)

ghost
if it is invisible under homology. Let
{\mathcal{S}}
denote the set
\{\mbox{S}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
of spherical chain complexes, where
\mbox{S}^{n}(R)
is the chain complex with
R
in the
n
-th position, and
0
elsewhere. A morphism
f
in
\mathbf{C}(R)
is ghost if and only if it is
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost; see Proposition
8.6
. We will investigate this ideal further in Section 8.

Recall that for a given subfunctor
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\rm Ext}
, a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{A}
is called an
\mathcal{F}
-
{\rm cophantom}
(see
[
17
]
) if the pushout of any conflation in
\mathcal{A}
along
f
is a conflation in

\mathcal{F}
. Such morphisms form the
\mathcal{F}
-
cophantom ideal
in
\mathcal{A}
. Every exact substructure
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
can be regarded as a subfunctor of
{\rm Ext},
in which case the ideal is denoted by
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
-
{\rm cophantom}.

Proposition 3.5
.

For a given set
{\mathcal{S}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, a morphism
f:X\to Y
is
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost if and only if it is
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-cophantom, i.e.,
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\mbox{-}{\rm cophantom}.

Proof.

If
f
is an
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-cophantom, then
{\rm Ext}(S,f)=0
for every
S\in{\mathcal{S}},
because
S
is
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-projective. For the converse, let
f\colon X\to Y
be an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost and consider a pushout of a conflation in
\mathcal{E}
along
f
as in

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C.}

For
S\in{\mathcal{S}},
apply the functor
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,-)
to the pushout diagram
to obtain the following commutative diagram of exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(S,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Ext}(S,f)}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Ext}(S,X)}

By hypothesis,
{\rm Ext}(S,f)=0
, so that

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(S,C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is an exact sequence, and hence
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}
belongs to
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}},
as required.
∎

If
\mathcal{A}
has coproducts, and
{\mathcal{S}}
is a generating set for
\mathcal{A}
, then by Proposition
2.2
, the exact structure
\mathcal{E}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
has enough projective objects. If we assume further that
\mathcal{A}
has enough projective objects, then it has been shown in
[
17
, Theorem 2]
that
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal. However, this result still holds even without these conditions.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
has exact coproducts
1
1

1

\mathcal{A}
is said to
have exact coproducts
if it has coproducts, and a coproduct of any conflations is conflation.
, then
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal with the class
{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
as a
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
-cosyzygy subcategory. More precisely, for any object
A\in\mathcal{A}
, there is a conflation

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W}

with
g
an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost map, and
W\in{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
.

Proof.

We follow an argument that appears in
[
19
]
with a subtle modification. Let
S
be the coproduct of all objects in the set
{\mathcal{S}}
and let
A
be an object in
\mathcal{A}
. By assumption, the coproduct of all conflations
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}
in
I={\rm Ext}(S,A)
exists, and is a conflation in
\mathcal{A}
. Consider the pushout diagram along the canonical morphism
A^{(I)}\to A

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.16913pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.16913pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{(I)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-26.0456pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
