0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.6584pt\raise-27.38239pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.09914pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.09914pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.89163pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 97.89163pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S^{(I)}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We shall show that
j:A\to G
belongs to the ideal
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
. This is equivalent to proving, that for any conflation
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}
, the pushout diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}

is null homotopic. The following commutative diagram of conflations

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{(I)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod_{i\in I}B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{(I)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{(I)}}

gives us the map
B_{i}\to G
such that the diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G}

commutes, as desired.
∎

In addition to the conditions given in Theorem
3.6
, if we assume further that
\mathcal{A}
has enough projective objects, then by
[
17
, Theorem 2]
, the pair
(^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
is a
complete ideal cotorsion pair
with
{}^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
an object ideal. In order to determine the objects of
{}^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
, we recall that for two subcategories
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{A}
, the subcategory consisting of objects
X
that fit into a conflation
B\to X\to C,
with
B\in\mathcal{B}
and
C\in\mathcal{C},
is denoted by
\mathcal{B}\star\mathcal{C}.
Applying
[
18
, Propositions 6.2 and 7.3]
, we obtain the following.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
has exact coproducts and enough projective objects, then
{}^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}=\langle{\rm add}(\mathcal{E}\mbox{-}{\rm Proj}\star{\rm Sum}({\mathcal{S}}))\rangle
is an object ideal.

Applying Proposition
3.1
and Theorem
3.6
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 3.8
.

let
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
has exact coproducts, then the
n
-th power
\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{\mathcal{S}}

is an object-special preenveloping ideal with
\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{\mathcal{S}}
-cosyzygy subcategory
n
-
\rm Filt({\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S}))
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
has enough projectives, then
(^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{\mathcal{S}})
is a complete ideal cotorison pair.

4.
Adjustment of splittings

As we have seen in Proposition
3.1
a finite power of an object special preenveloping ideal is still an object-special preenveloping ideal. In the next few sections, we will push forward, that is, we will define transfinite inductive powers of an ideal, and show that under very mild conditions on the exact category
\mathcal{A},
a transfinite inductive power of an object special preenveloping ideal is still an object-special preenveloping ideal. As in the corollary above, this will immediately apply to the
\mathcal{S}
-ghost ideal. In order to carry this out, we need to generalize Eklof’s Lemma, an important pillar in classical approximation theory, to the present setting. We call this generalization the
Ideal Eklof Lemma
. We will begin with a very useful technique
the adjustment of splittings,
which will play an important role in the proof of the Ideal Eklof Lemma, and may be of independent interest.

If
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
is an exact category, then the morphisms of conflations in
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
themselves form an exact category
({\rm Arr}(\mathcal{A});{\rm Arr}(\mathcal{E})).
The notation
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{z}
is used to represent the conflation of arrows given by

\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Z_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{Z_{1}.}

In this section, we consider conditions under which a morphism of trivial conflations in
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
is itself trivial when regarded as a conflation in
({\rm Arr}(\mathcal{A});{\rm Arr}(\mathcal{E})).
This involves the introduction of a splitting for each of the conflations of objects and a study of the criteria for when those splittings may be adjusted to yield a splitting of arrows. These ideas are best understood when the conflations are considered as complexes in
{\bf C}(\mathcal{A}),
so that we will follow horizontal indexing conventions in this section, rather than the usual vertical ones.

4.1.
The connecting map

Let us consider conflations in
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E})
as complexes and work in the
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}
-complex of maps. Concretely, a conflation
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{-1}}
may be regarded as the object
X.

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{X}_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{X}_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

in
{\bf C}(\mathcal{A}),
concentrated in degrees
1,

0,
and
-1.
If
Y.
is another conflation in
(\mathcal{A};\mathcal{E}),
the complex
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}.(X.,Y.)
is defined so that the
n
-chains are maps of degree
n,

{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{n}(X.,Y.)=\prod_{i}\;{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(X_{i},Y_{i+n}).

Since
X.
and
Y.
are conflations,
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{n}(X.,Y.)=0
for
|n|>2
, and therefore, the chain complex
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}.(X.,Y.)
looks like

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(X.,Y.)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{2}}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{1}(X.,Y.)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{1}}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{0}(X.,Y.)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{0}}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{-1}(X.,Y.)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D_{-1}}
\textstyle{{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{-2}(X.,Y.)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The differential
D_{n}
is defined so that its
i
-th component acts on
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(X_{i},Y_{i+n})
by

[D_{n}(f)]_{i}=d_{i+n}^{Y}\circ f_{i}-(-1)^{n}f_{i-1}\circ d_{i}^{X}.

We are interested in degree
2
maps
\Delta\in{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(X.,Y.)

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1},}

which we call
connecting.
The boundary of a connecting map is given by
D_{2}(\Delta)=(-\Delta p_{X},i_{Y}\Delta).

Lemma 4.1
.

For given conflations
X.
and
Y.
in
\mathcal{A}
, the chain complex
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}.(X.,Y.)
is exact at
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(X.,Y.)
and
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{1}(X.,Y.).
In other words, a degree
1
map
h=(h_{0},h_{-1})
of conflations

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{h_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{-1}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}}

is a homotopy of
0,
i.e.,
D_{1}(h)=0
, if and only if there exists a unique connecting map
\Delta\colon X_{-1}\to Y_{1}
such that
h_{0}=-\Delta p_{X}
and
h_{-1}=i_{Y}\Delta,
i.e.,
D_{2}(\Delta)=h.

Proof.

If
\Delta\colon X_{-1}\to Y_{1}
is a connecting map, then the degree
1
map
D_{2}(\Delta)=(-\Delta p_{X},i_{Y}\Delta)
is a
1
-boundary and therefore a
1
-cycle, i.e., a homotopy of
0.

Conversely, suppose that
h=(h_{0},h_{-1})
is a homotopy of
0,

D_{1}(h)=0.
Then
h_{0}i_{X}=0,

i_{Y}h_{0}+h_{-1}p_{X}=0,
and
p_{Y}h_{-1}=0.

The first equation implies there is a
unique

\Delta\colon X_{-1}\to Y_{1}
such that
h_{0}=-\Delta p_{X}
and the third equation implies there is a unique
\Delta^{\prime}\colon X_{-1}\to Y_{1}
such that
h_{-1}=i_{Y}\Delta^{\prime}.

Substituting these equations into the second yields

0=i_{Y}h_{0}+h_{-1}p_{X}=-i_{Y}\Delta p_{X}+i_{Y}\Delta^{\prime}p_{X}=i_{Y}(-\Delta+\Delta^{\prime})p_{X}.

As
i_{Y}
is a kernel and
p_{X}
a cokernel, they are a monomorphism
and an epimorphism respectively, whence
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta
and

h=(h_{0},h_{-1})=(-\Delta p_{X},i_{Y}\Delta)=D_{2}(\Delta).

The uniqueness of
\Delta
ensures exactness of
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}.(X.,Y.)
at
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(X.,Y.).

∎

4.2.
Splittings

A
splitting

(r,s)
of a trivial conflation
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X_{-1}}
in
\mathcal{A}
is a degree
1
map for which
D_{1}(r,s)=1_{X.}.
In other words, it is a self-homotopy of the identity morphism
1_{X.},

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X_{-1},}

which consists of a retraction
r:X_{0}\to X_{1}
of
i,

ri=1_{X_{1}},
and a section
s:X_{-1}\to X_{0}
of
p,

ps=1_{X_{1}},
satisfying
1_{X_{0}}=ir+sp.
The splitting is uniquely determined by the section
r
or the retration
s
, that is, once a retraction
r
is given, then there is a unique section
s
induced by
r
such that
(r,s)
is a splitting, and vice versa.

Proposition 4.2
.

If
(r,s)
is a splitting of
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X_{-1},}
then every splitting of
X.
is of the form
(r,s)-D_{2}(\Delta)=(r+\Delta p,s-i\Delta)
for some connecting map
\Delta\colon X_{-1}\to X_{1}.

Proof.

Suppose that
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{1}(X.,X.)
is another splitting,
D_{1}(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})=1_{X.}.
Then
D_{1}(r-r^{\prime},s-s^{\prime})=0
, and therefore, by Lemma
4.1
, there exists a connecting map
\Delta
such that
(r-r^{\prime},s-s^{\prime})=D_{2}(\Delta)=(-p\Delta,\Delta i),
whence
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})=(r+p\Delta,s-\Delta i).

∎

A given splitting
(r,s)
yields an isomorphism of kernel-cokernel pairs

\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\end{array}\right]
\textstyle{X_{-1}\oplus X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[0,1]}
\scriptstyle{[s,i]}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{X_{1},}

because the morphism
[s,i]\colon X_{-1}\oplus X_{1}\to X_{0}
is an isomorphism with inverse
{\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}p\\
r\end{array}\right]\colon X_{0}\to X_{-1}\oplus X_{1}.}

By
[
8
, Lemma 2.7]
, the kernel-cokernel pair in the bottom row is a trivial conflation with a splitting
(p,i).

Let
f\colon X.\to Y.
be a morphism between two trivial conflations in
\mathcal{A}
, displayed as a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{-1}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}.}

Equivalently,
f\in{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{0}(X.,Y.)
is a
0
-cycle. Let
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y})
be splittings of
X.
and
Y.
and consider
f
as a degree
0
map of reversed conflations

\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{-1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}.}

If the diagram is commutative, we say that the splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y})
are a splitting for the conflation
f
of arrows. In any case, we may consider its
-1
-boundary
(s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X},r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X})
as a degree
1
map

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}.}

Let us show that it is a homotopy of
0.

Proposition 4.3
.

Given a morphism
f\colon X.\to Y.
of trivial conflations, equipped with splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y})
as above, there is a unique connecting map
\Delta=:\Delta(f)
such that

D_{2}(\Delta)=(r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X},s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X}).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
, it suffices to verify that
(r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X},s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X})
is a
1
-cycle, that is,
D_{1}(r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X},s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X})=0
. First,

(r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X})i_{X}=r_{Y}f_{0}i_{X}-f_{1}r_{X}i_{X}=r_{Y}f_{0}i_{X}-f_{1}=0,

because
i_{Y}(r_{Y}f_{0}i_{X}-f_{1})=i_{Y}r_{Y}f_{0}i_{X}-i_{Y}f_{1}=(i_{Y}r_{Y}-1_{Y_{0}})(i_{Y}f_{1})=s_{Y}p_{Y}(i_{Y}f_{1})=0
and
i_{Y}
is a monomorphism. Then,

\displaystyle i_{Y}(r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X})+(s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X})p_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{Y}r_{Y}f_{0}-i_{Y}f_{1}r_{X}+s_{Y}f_{-1}p_{X}-f_{0}s_{X}p_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{Y}r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{0}i_{X}r_{X}+s_{Y}p_{Y}f_{0}-f_{0}s_{X}p_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i_{Y}r_{Y}+s_{Y}p_{Y})f_{0}-f_{0}(i_{X}r_{X}+s_{X}p_{X})=0.

The verification of the third equation
p_{Y}(s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X})=0
is similar to the first.
∎

We call the connecting map obtained in Proposition
4.3
the
connecting map of

f\colon X.\to Y.
By the proof of Lemma
4.1
, it is uniquely determined by either of the equations

(2)

-\Delta(f)p_{X}=r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X}\;\;\mbox{ or }\;\;i_{Y}\Delta(f)=s_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{0}s_{X}.

This can be used to verify the following.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose that
\textstyle{X.\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Z.}
is a composition of morphisms of trivial conflations,

\textstyle{X.:\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{-1}}
\textstyle{Y.:\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{g_{0}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{-1}}
\textstyle{Z.:\;}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Z}}
\textstyle{Z_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Z}}
\textstyle{Z_{-1},}

equipped with respective splittings
(r_{X},s_{X}),

(r_{Y},s_{Y}),
and
(r_{Z},s_{Z}).
Then
\Delta(gf)=\Delta(g)f_{-1}+g_{1}\Delta(f).

4.3.
Adjustment of splittings

If
f\colon X.\to Y.
is a morphism of trivial conflations, equipped with splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y}),
respectively, we know from Proposition
4.2
that every splitting of
X.
is of the form

(r_{X},s_{X})-D_{2}(\Delta_{X})
for some
\Delta_{X}\in{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(X.,X.),
and likewise for
(r_{Y},s_{Y}).
The next theorem uses the connecting map
\Delta(f)
to give a necessary and sufficient condition for
f
to be a trivial arrow conflation.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
f\colon X.\to Y.
be a morphism of trivial conflations, equipped with splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y}),
respectively. The conflation of arrows
\textstyle{f_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{f_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{f_{-1}}
is trival in
\operatorname{Arr}(\mathcal{A})
if and only if there exist
\Delta_{X}\in{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(X.,X.)
and
\Delta_{Y}\in{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{2}(Y.,Y.)
such that

\Delta(f)=\Delta_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{1}\Delta_{X}.

In that case, the splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})-D_{2}(\Delta_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y})-D_{2}(\Delta_{Y})
give a splitting for
f
in
{\rm Arr}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

Suppose the condition holds and let us verify that
f
is a morphism of the reverse complexes associated to the adjusted splittings, that is, that the diagram

\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{X}-i_{X}\Delta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{-1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{X}+\Delta_{X}p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{Y}-i_{Y}\Delta_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{Y}+\Delta_{Y}p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}}

commutes. We only verify commutativity of the right diagram; verification of the left one is similar. Recall that
r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X}=-\Delta(f)p_{X},
whence

\displaystyle f_{1}(r_{X}+\Delta_{X}p_{X})=f_{1}r_{X}+f_{1}\Delta_{X}p_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{Y}f_{0}+\Delta(f)p_{X}+f_{1}\Delta_{X}p_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{Y}f_{0}+\Delta_{Y}f_{-1}p_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{Y}f_{0}+\Delta_{Y}p_{Y}f_{0}=(r_{Y}+\Delta_{Y}p_{Y})f_{0}.

To prove the converse, assume the conflation is trivial. By Proposition
4.2
, there exist connection maps
\Delta_{X}\colon X_{-1}\to X_{1}
and
\Delta_{Y}\colon Y_{-1}\to Y_{1}
such that the splittings

(r_{X}+\Delta_{X}p_{X},s_{X}-i_{X}\Delta_{X})
and
(r_{Y}+\Delta_{Y}p_{Y},s_{Y}-i_{Y}\Delta_{Y})
of
X.
and
Y.,
respectively, make the diagram commute. To verify that
\Delta(f)=\Delta_{X}f_{-1}-f_{1}\Delta_{X},
use the commutativity of the right square

f_{1}(r_{X}+\Delta_{X}p_{X})=(r_{Y}+\Delta_{Y}p_{Y})f_{0}
to get that

-(\Delta_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{1}\Delta_{X})p_{X}=-\Delta_{Y}(f_{-1}p_{X})+f_{1}\Delta_{X}p_{X}=-\Delta_{Y}p_{Y}f_{0}+f_{1}\Delta_{X}p_{X}=r_{Y}f_{0}-f_{1}r_{X}.

It means that
\Delta_{Y}f_{-1}-f_{1}\Delta_{X}
is a solution to the first of Equations (
2
) and therefore equals
\Delta(f).

∎

In practice, Theorem
4.5
will be used for
downward adjustment of splittings
of arrows (see Definition
5.8
). This means that the splitting
(r_{X},s_{X})
stays fixed and
(r_{Y},s_{Y})
is adjusted. In other words, we are faced with the constraint that
\Delta_{X}=0
and the task of finding a morphism

\Delta_{Y}\colon Y_{-1}\to Y_{1}
such that
\Delta(f)=\Delta_{Y}f_{-1},

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{-1}}
\textstyle{Y_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y_{-1}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{Y}}

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
f\colon X.\to Y.
be a morphism of trivial conflations in
\mathcal{A}
, equipped with respective splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y}).
If the connecting map
\Delta(f):X_{-1}\to Y_{1}
extends along
f_{-1}
to
\Delta_{Y}\colon Y_{-1}\to Y_{1},

\Delta(f)=\Delta_{Y}f_{-1},
then the splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Y},s_{Y})-D_{2}(\Delta_{Y})
provide a splitting for
f.

Corollary
4.6
allows us to handle a situation that will arise in the sequel in the following way.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
f\colon X.\to Z.
be a morphism of trivial conflations that admits a factorization
\textstyle{f\colon X.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{Y.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Z.}
of the form

\textstyle{X.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{-1}}
\textstyle{Y.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z.}
\textstyle{Z_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{Z}}
\textstyle{Z_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Z}}
\textstyle{Z_{-1},}

with
Y.
a trivial conflation. If
h_{-1}
is an inflation satisfying
{\rm Ext}({\rm Coker}(h_{-1}),g_{1})=0,
then for every splitting
(r_{X},s_{X})
of
X.
, there exists a splitting
(r_{Z},s_{Z})
of
Z.

that yields a splitting for
f.

Proof.

Let
(r_{X},s_{X})
be a given splitting of
X.
and fix any splitting
(r_{Y},s_{Y})
of
Y.
. Because we have that

{\rm Ext}({\rm Coker}(h_{-1}),g_{1}\Delta(h))={\rm Ext}({\rm Coker}(h_{-1}),g_{1}){\rm Ext}({\rm Coker}(h_{-1}),\Delta(h))=0,
the morphism
g_{1}\Delta(h)\colon X_{-1}\to Z_{1}
extends along the inflation
h_{-1}
as in

\textstyle{X_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta(h)}
\textstyle{Z_{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{Z}}
\textstyle{{\rm Coker}(h_{-1})}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{Z_{1},}

where the top row is a conflation. Now let
(r_{Z},s_{Z})
be an arbitrary splitting of
Z.
and apply Lemma
4.4
to get that

\Delta(f)=\Delta(gh)=\Delta(g)h_{-1}+g_{1}\Delta(h)=\Delta(g)h_{-1}+\Delta_{Z}h_{-1}=(\Delta(g)+\Delta_{Z})h_{-1}.

By Corollary
4.6
, the splittings
(r_{X},s_{X})
and
(r_{Z},s_{Z})-D_{2}(\Delta(g)+\Delta_{Z})
give a splitting for
f.

∎

5.
The Ideal Eklof Lemma

In this section, we aim at proving The Ideal Eklof Lemma (Theorem
5.13
), which will provide us with a crucial tool for proving our main result in Section 6. We start by recalling some necessary terminology and notation.

5.1.
The category of directed
\lambda
-systems

Let
\lambda
be an ordinal. We consider it as a category from its canonical partial order. We let
(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
denote the category of functors from
\lambda
to
\mathcal{A}
. A functor
{\sf A}\colon\lambda\to\mathcal{A}
is in fact a directed
\lambda
-system
\mathsf{A}:=(A_{\alpha},a^{\alpha}_{\beta}\ |\ \alpha<\beta<\lambda)
which can be displayed as

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{0}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{a^{0}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{a^{0}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{a^{0}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{1}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{a^{1}_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{A_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{A_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots.}

The
composition
of the
\lambda
-system
\mathsf{A}
in
\mathcal{A}
, if it exists, is the canonical colimit morphism in
\mathcal{A}

A_{0}\longrightarrow\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}A_{\alpha}.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\lambda
be an ordinal. A
\lambda
-system
{\sf A}\colon\lambda\to\mathcal{A}
is said to be continuous at the limit ordinal
\beta<\lambda
if
\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\beta}}A_{\alpha}
exists, and
the canonical morphism

a_{\beta}\colon\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\beta}}A_{\alpha}\longrightarrow A_{\beta}

is an isomorphism. It is said to be continuous if it is continuous at every limit ordinal
\gamma<\lambda.
The subcategory of continuous
\lambda
-systems in
\mathcal{A}
is denoted by
{\rm Cont}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\subseteq(\lambda,\mathcal{A}).

Note that a continuous
\lambda
-system
{\sf A}\colon\lambda\to\mathcal{A}
with composition morphism
{\displaystyle A_{0}\longrightarrow\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}A_{\alpha}}
gives rise to a continuous
(\lambda+1)
-system
(A_{\alpha},a^{\alpha}_{\beta}\ |\ \alpha<\beta\leq\lambda)
, where
A_{\lambda}:=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}A_{\alpha}
, and
a_{\lambda}^{\alpha}:A_{\alpha}\longrightarrow A_{\lambda}
is the canonical colimit morphism for every
\alpha<\lambda
.
Conversely, any continuous
(\lambda+1)
-system
\mathsf{A}
in
\mathcal{A}
is of the form
(A_{\alpha},a^{\alpha}_{\beta}\ |\ \alpha<\beta\leq\lambda)
with composition morphism
a_{\lambda}^{0}:A_{0}\longrightarrow A_{\lambda}
.

For a given continuous
(\lambda+1)
-system
{\sf A}\colon\lambda+1\to\mathcal{A}
, if the morphisms
a^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}
,
\alpha<\lambda
, belong to some class
\mathcal{J}
of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
, the composition morphism
a^{0}_{\lambda}
is said to be a
\lambda
-
transfinite composition of morphisms in

\mathcal{J}
, or simply a
\lambda
-
composition of morphisms in

\mathcal{J}.
We say that
\mathcal{J}
has
\lambda
-compositions
if every continuous
\lambda
-system in
\mathcal{J}
has a composition morphism in
\mathcal{A}
.

The following proposition is immediate as (co)limits in functor categories can be calculated object-wise.

Proposition 5.2
.

An exact structure
\mathcal{E}
on
\mathcal{A}
induces the exact structure
(\lambda,\mathcal{E})
on
(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
whose conflations are the directed
\lambda
-systems of conflations (or just
\lambda
-conflations) in
\mathcal{E}.

A conflation in
((\lambda,\mathcal{A});(\lambda,\mathcal{E}))
, denoted by
\mathsf{\Xi}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.49724pt\raise 5.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.8167pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C},}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

may be called a
\lambda
-conflation and is displayed as a
\lambda
-system
{(\ \Xi_{\alpha},\xi_{\beta}^{\alpha}\mid\ \alpha<\beta<\lambda\ )}
of conflations in
\mathcal{A},
where
\xi_{\beta}^{\alpha}:\Xi_{\alpha}\to\Xi_{\beta}
is a morphism of conflations

\textstyle{\Xi_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces:\;A_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{\alpha}_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{a^{\alpha}_{\beta}}
\textstyle{B_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{b^{\alpha}_{\beta}}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\alpha}_{\beta}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{\beta}:\;A_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\beta}}
\textstyle{B_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\beta}}
\textstyle{C_{\beta}.}

A
\lambda
-conflation
\mathsf{\Xi}
is
continuous
if its objects belong to
{\rm Cont}(\lambda,\mathcal{E}).

As in the exact category
\mathcal{A},
a
\lambda
-conflation
\mathsf{\Xi}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.49724pt\raise 5.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.12224pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
of
\lambda
-systems is
trivial
provided there exists a splitting

\mathsf{\Xi}^{-}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.19444pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.19444pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 11.92223pt\raise 4.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{s}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.19444pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.19444pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.6653pt\raise 4.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{r}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.86113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 60.86113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
such that
\mathsf{r}\mathsf{i}=1_{\mathsf{A}},

\mathsf{p}\mathsf{s}=1_{\mathsf{C}}
and

\mathsf{i}\mathsf{r}+\mathsf{s}\mathsf{p}=1_{\mathsf{B}}.
Equivalently, the
\lambda
-conflation
\mathsf{\Xi}^{-}
consists of a family
\{(r_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
of splittings of the
\Xi_{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{A}
such that for every
\alpha<\beta<\lambda,

((r_{\alpha},s_{\alpha}),(r_{\beta},s_{\beta}))
is a splitting of the conflation
\xi_{\beta}^{\alpha}
in
({\rm Arr}(\mathcal{A});{\rm Arr}(\mathcal{E})).

The following lemma is the limit step in the transfinite induction of the proof of Theorem
5.9
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\lambda
be a limit ordinal and
\mathsf{\Xi}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.49724pt\raise 5.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.12224pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
be a
(\lambda+1)
-conflation such that
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda}\in(\lambda,\mathcal{E})
is trivial. If
{\sf A}
and
{\sf C}
are continuous at
\lambda,
then
\mathsf{\Xi}
is continuous at
\lambda
and trivial. Furthermore, every splitting
(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda})^{-}
extends to one of
\mathsf{\Xi},
that is,
(\mathsf{\Xi}^{-})|_{\lambda}=(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda})^{-}.

Proof.

The
\lambda
-systems
{\sf A}
and
{\sf C}
are continuous at
\lambda,
so that the direct sum
{\sf A}\oplus{\sf C}
is as well. Let
(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda})^{-}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.20488pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.20488pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.93266pt\raise 4.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{s}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.20488pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.20488pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.69658pt\raise 4.55556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.55556pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{r}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.89241pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.89241pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}|_{\lambda}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
be a splitting for
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda}.
Because
\mathsf{(i|_{\lambda})r+s(p|_{\lambda})=1_{{\sf B}|_{\lambda}}},
the morphism of
\lambda
-conflations

\textstyle{\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda}\colon}
\textstyle{{\sf A}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i|_{\lambda}}}
\textstyle{{\sf B}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p|_{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{r}\\
\mathsf{p}\end{array}\right]}
\textstyle{{\sf C}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\sf A}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{(i_{A})|_{\lambda}}}
\textstyle{{\sf A}|_{\lambda}\oplus{\sf C}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{(p_{C})|_{\lambda}}}
\textstyle{{\sf C}|_{\lambda}}

is an isomorphism. Since the
\lambda
-conflation in the bottom row has a limit at
\lambda,
so does the one in the top row. By the universal property of the limit, we have a morphism of
\lambda
-conflations

\textstyle{\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}\Xi_{\alpha}\colon\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}A_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lim(i_{\alpha})}
\scriptstyle{a_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}B_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lim(p_{\alpha})}
\scriptstyle{b_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\;\;\;\Xi_{\lambda}\colon}
\textstyle{A_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{B_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{C_{\lambda},}

where, because
{\sf A}
and
{\sf C}
are continuous at
\lambda,
the morphisms
a_{\lambda}
and
c_{\lambda}
are isomorphisms. This implies that this morphism of conflations is an isomorphism and that
\mathsf{\Xi}
is also continuous at
\lambda.

Finally, to extend the splitting
(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda})^{-}
to include
\lambda
just take the limit at
\lambda
of the isomorphism of
\lambda
-conflations

\textstyle{(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda})^{-}\colon}
\textstyle{{\sf C}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{s}}
\textstyle{{\sf B}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{r}}
\scriptstyle{\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{p}|_{\lambda}\\
\mathsf{r}|_{\lambda}\end{array}\right]}
\textstyle{{\sf A}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\sf C}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{(i_{C})|_{\lambda}}}
\textstyle{{\sf C}|_{\lambda}\oplus{\sf A}|_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{(p_{A})|_{\lambda}}}
\textstyle{{\sf A}|_{\lambda}.}

∎

5.2.
Transfinite powers of an ideal

In order to state the Ideal Eklof Lemma, we need to introduce (transfinite) inductive powers of an ideal
\mathcal{J}
in
\mathcal{A}
. Recall that if
n
is a positive integer, the
n
-th power
\mathcal{J}^{n}
of
\mathcal{J}
is the ideal generated by all compositions
a_{n}a_{n-1}\cdots a_{1}
with
a_{i}\in\mathcal{J}
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
. In other words, it is generated by all
n
-compositions of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
. In a similar way, for any ordinal
\lambda>0
, we define the
\lambda
-th inductive power of

\mathcal{J}
, denoted by
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
, as the ideal generated by all
\lambda
-compositions of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
.

In the following result, we provide a characterization of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
. We will use the fact that if
{\sf A}
and
\mathsf{A^{\prime}}
are continuous
(\lambda+1)
-systems, then so is
\mathsf{A}\oplus\mathsf{A^{\prime}}.

Proposition 5.4
.

A morphism
g
in
\mathcal{A}
belongs to
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
if and only if
g
is a composition of the form
ha_{\lambda}^{0}
, where
a_{\lambda}^{0}:A_{0}\longrightarrow A_{\lambda}
is a
\lambda
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
denote the class of all morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
of the form
ha_{\lambda}^{0}
, where
a_{\lambda}^{0}
is a
\lambda
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
. By the definition of an ideal,
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
, so it is enough to prove that
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
is an ideal in
\mathcal{A}.
It is obviously closed under left multiplication, but it is also closed under right multiplication. Indeed, if
ha^{0}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
with
a^{0}_{\lambda}
a
\lambda
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J},
then for any morphism
t,

ha^{0}_{\lambda}t=ha^{1}_{\lambda}(a^{0}_{1}t).
As
a^{0}_{1}t\in\mathcal{J},
this morphism also belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}.

Now, it is left to prove that
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
is additive, that is, if
ha_{\lambda}^{0},h^{\prime}{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}:A_{0}\longrightarrow B
belong to
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
, then so does
ha_{\lambda}^{0}+h^{\prime}{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}:A_{0}\longrightarrow B
. Let
\mathsf{A}:=(A_{\alpha},a_{\beta}^{\alpha}\mid\ \alpha<\beta\leq\lambda)
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}:=(A^{\prime}_{\alpha},{a^{\prime}}_{\beta}^{\alpha}\mid\ \alpha<\beta\leq\lambda)
be two
(\lambda+1)
-sequences in
\mathcal{A}
with the composition morphisms
a_{\lambda}^{0}
and
{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}
, respectively, satisfying
a_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha},{a^{\prime}}_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{J}
for every
\alpha<\lambda
. Since both the colimit functor and
\mathcal{J}
are additive, the morphism
[a_{\lambda}^{0},{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}]:A_{0}\oplus A_{0}\longrightarrow A_{\lambda}\oplus A^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is a
(\lambda+1)
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}

of the
(\lambda+1)
-sequence
\mathsf{A}\oplus\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
defined by

(A_{\alpha}\oplus A^{\prime}_{\alpha},[a_{\beta}^{\alpha},{a^{\prime}}_{\beta}^{\alpha}]\mid\ \alpha<\beta\leq\lambda).

Consider the (co)diagonal morphisms
\varsigma:A_{0}\longrightarrow A_{0}\oplus A_{0}
and
\rho:B\oplus B\longrightarrow B
. By the previous argument, the composition
[a_{\lambda}^{0},{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}]\varsigma

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{\lambda}^{0}\\
{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}\end{array}\right]=[a_{\lambda}^{0},{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}]\varsigma:A_{0}\longrightarrow A_{\lambda}\oplus A^{\prime}_{\lambda}

is a
(\lambda+1)
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
. Finally, the morphism

ha_{\lambda}^{0}+h{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}=\rho[h,h^{\prime}]\left[\begin{array}[]{c}a_{\lambda}^{0}\\
{a^{\prime}}_{\lambda}^{0}\end{array}\right]

belongs to
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
.
∎

Proposition 5.5
.

Given an ideal
\mathcal{J}
and ordinals
\alpha
and
\beta,

\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}\mathcal{J}^{(\beta)}=\mathcal{J}^{(\beta+\alpha)}.

Proof.

To prove
\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}\mathcal{J}^{(\beta)}\subseteq\mathcal{J}^{(\beta+\alpha)}
, note that a composition
ha^{0}_{\alpha}gb^{0}_{\beta}=ha^{1}_{\alpha}(a^{0}_{1}g)b^{0}_{\beta}
is a
(\beta+\alpha)
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
post-composed with
h.
For the converse inclusion, just factor
ha^{0}_{\beta+\alpha}=ha^{\beta}_{\beta+\alpha}a^{0}_{\beta}.

∎

The morphisms in the
\lambda
-th inductive power of a successor ordinal are even simpler.

Proposition 5.6
.

If
\lambda=\gamma+1
is a successor ordinal, then
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{J}^{(\gamma)}
consists of
\lambda
-compositions of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

A composition
ha^{0}_{\lambda}=(ha^{\gamma}_{\gamma+1})a^{0}_{\gamma}
is a
(\gamma+1)
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}.

∎

In particular, we have the following.

Proposition 5.7
.

If
n<\omega,
then
\mathcal{J}^{n}=\mathcal{J}^{(n)}
consists of
n
-compositions of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}.

5.3.
From local triviality to global triviality

Every trivial conflation of
\lambda
-systems is evidently a
\lambda
-system of trivial conflations. In this subsection, we present a condition that ensures a
\lambda
-system of trivial conflations is a trivial conflation of
\lambda
-systems. Here is one of the main ideas of the paper.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
\lambda
be a limit ordinal. A
\lambda
-system
\mathsf{\Xi}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.49724pt\raise 5.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.12224pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
of trivial conflations is said to have the downward adjustment property if for every
\alpha<\lambda,

and every splitting
(r_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
of
\Xi_{\alpha},
there exists a splitting
(r_{\alpha+1},s_{\alpha+1})
of
\Xi_{\alpha+1}
that provides a splitting for the arrow conflation

\textstyle{\xi^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}\colon a^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}.}

Theorem 5.9
.

(From local triviality to global triviality)

Let
\lambda
be a limit ordinal and suppose that
\mathsf{\Xi}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.49724pt\raise 5.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.12224pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
is a
\lambda
-system of trivial conflations with the downward adjustment property.
If
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{C}
are continuous, then
\mathsf{\Xi}
is trivial and continuous.

Proof.

Let us define a splitting
\mathsf{\Xi}^{-}
of
\mathsf{\Xi}
by recursion on the ordinals
\alpha<\lambda
so that
(\mathsf{\Xi}^{-})|_{\alpha}
is a splitting for
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\alpha}.
Because
\Xi_{0}
is a trivial conflation in
\mathcal{A},
define
(r_{0},s_{0})
to be any splitting of
\Xi_{0}.
For a successor ordinal
\alpha=\beta+1,
assume that the system
\mathsf{\Xi}^{-}
has been defined for
\gamma\leq\beta,
with
\Xi^{-}_{\beta}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.44844pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.44844pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.39078pt\raise 5.5236pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.4903pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s_{\beta}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.44844pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.44844pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.9267pt\raise 5.5236pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.4903pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r_{\beta}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.56966pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.56966pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A_{\beta}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
By the downward adjustment property of
\mathsf{\Xi},
there is a splitting
(r_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
such that the two splittings provide one for the arrow conflation
\xi^{\beta}_{\alpha}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.49232pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.49232pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{a^{\beta}_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.49232pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.49232pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b^{\beta}_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.48274pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.48274pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{c^{\beta}_{\alpha}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.
Define
\Xi^{-}_{\alpha}
to be the conflation
\textstyle{C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{B_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{A_{\alpha}.}

For the case when
\alpha
is a limit ordinal, we use Lemma
5.3
. We have up until now defined as splitting
(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\alpha})^{-}
for
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\alpha}.
Because
{\sf A}
and
{\sf C}
are continuous at
\alpha,
the lemma applies to the

(\alpha+1)
-conflation
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\alpha+1}
and we can extend the splitting
(\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\alpha})^{-}
to one of
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\alpha+1}.

∎

5.4.
Eklof’s Lemma

Let us use the results of the previous subsection to give a proof of a general version of Eklof’s Lemma (Corollary
5.12
) in an exact category.

Definition 5.10
.

A continuous
\lambda
-system
{\sf A}
is said to be a
\lambda
-filtration if
A_{0}=0
and all of the successive structural morphisms
a^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1},

\alpha+1<\lambda,
are inflations.
If all of the cokernels
{\rm Coker}(a^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1})
belong to some subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{A},
then
{\sf A}
is called a
\lambda
-
\mathcal{C}
-
filtration
.
An object
C\in\mathcal{A}
is said to
have a

\lambda
-
filtration in

\mathcal{C}
if there exists a
(\lambda+1)
-
\mathcal{C}
-filtration
{\sf C}
with
C_{\lambda}=C.
The subcategory of objects having a
\lambda
-
\mathcal{C}
-filtration is denoted by
\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mathcal{C}).
We let
\rm Filt(\mathcal{C})
denote the subcategory of objects having a
\lambda
-
\mathcal{C}
-filtration for some ordinal
\lambda
.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
\lambda
be a limit ordinal and suppose that
\mathsf{\Xi}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.49724pt\raise 5.37778pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.33334pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.12224pt\raise 5.2361pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.875pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.00003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{C}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
is a
\lambda
-system of trivial conflations with
\mathsf{A}
continuous and
\mathsf{C}
a
\lambda
-filtration in
\mathcal{A}.
If for every ordinal
\alpha
with
\alpha+1<\lambda
,
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1},A_{\alpha+1})
is an epimorphism, then
\mathsf{\Xi}
has the downward adjustment property.

Proof.

Consider the arrow conflation obtained from the successive structural morphisms of the three
\lambda
-systems,

\textstyle{\Xi_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces:\;A_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{a^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{B_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{b^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{\alpha+1}:\;A_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{B_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha+1},}

and choose splittings
(r_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
and
(r_{\alpha+1},s_{\alpha+1})
of the respective trivial conflations
\Xi_{\alpha}
and
\Xi_{\alpha+1}.
By hypothesis, the induced connecting map
\Delta(\xi^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1})\colon C_{\alpha}\to A_{\alpha+1}
may be extended along
c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}
as shown,

\textstyle{C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta(\xi^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1})}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha+1}/C_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{A_{\alpha+1}.}

Corollary
4.6
may therefore by employed to adjust the given splitting
(r_{\alpha+1},s_{\alpha+1})
to a splitting
(r^{\prime}_{\alpha+1},s^{\prime}_{\alpha+1})
such that
(r_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
and
(r^{\prime}_{\alpha+1},s^{\prime}_{\alpha+1})
gives a splitting of the arrow conflation
\xi^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}.

∎

Corollary 5.12
.

(Eklof’s Lemma)

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a class of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. If an object
C
in
\mathcal{A}
has a
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{M}
-filtration, then
C\in{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{M}.

Proof.

Let
{\sf C}
be a
(\lambda+1)
-
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{M}
-filtration with
C_{\lambda}=C
. We shall show, by induction on
\lambda,
that any conflation
\Xi:A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
in
{\rm Ext}(C,A)
with
A\in\mathcal{M}
is trivial. If
\lambda=0,
then
C=C_{0}=0.
If
\lambda=\gamma+1
is a successor, then there is a conflation
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.2815pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.2815pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.2815pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.2815pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.14398pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.14398pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C/C_{\gamma}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.
By assumption,
C_{\gamma}
and
C/C_{\gamma}
belong to
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{M},
and hence
C
belongs to
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{M}.

If
\lambda
is a limit ordinal, take the pullback of
\Xi
along
{\sf C}
to obtain the
(\lambda+1)
-conflation
\mathsf{\Xi},

\textstyle{\Xi_{0}\colon\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{1}^{0}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{\beta}^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{C_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{1}^{0}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{1}\colon\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{2}^{1}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{2}^{1}}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{2}^{1}}
\textstyle{\vdots\;\;\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Xi\colon}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C,}

where
\Xi_{\lambda}=\Xi.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda}
is a
\lambda
-system of trivial conflations. We will apply Theorem
5.11
to show that
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda}
has the downward adjustment property. Then Theorem
5.9
will apply to show that
\mathsf{\Xi}|_{\lambda}
is trivial. Because both of the outside
(\lambda+1)
-systems are continuous at
\lambda,
Lemma
5.3
will apply to show that
\mathsf{\Xi}
and therefore
\Xi=\Xi_{\lambda}
is trivial.

To apply Theorem
5.11
, it suffices to verify that
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1},A)\colon{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(C_{\alpha+1},A)\to{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(C_{\alpha},A)
is epic for very
\alpha<\lambda
. But the hypothesis
{\rm Ext}(C_{\alpha+1}/C_{\alpha},A)=0
ensures that every morphism
f\colon C_{\alpha}\to A
extends as in

\textstyle{C_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{\alpha+1}/C_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{A.}

∎

5.5.
The Ideal Eklof Lemma

Now we are in a position to state and prove the Ideal Eklof Lemma.

Theorem 5.13
.

(The Ideal Eklof Lemma)

Let
\mathcal{J}
be an ideal of
\mathcal{A}.
If an object
C
in
\mathcal{A}
has a
\lambda
-filtration in
\operatorname{Ob}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J}),
then
C\in{{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)})}.

Proof.

Let
{\sf C}
be an
(\lambda+1)
-
\mbox{Ob}({{}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}})
-filtration with
C=C_{\lambda},
and
{\rm Ext}(C_{\alpha+1}/C_{\alpha},j)=0
for all morphisms
j
in
\mathcal{J}
and ordinals
\alpha<\lambda.
We prove that
C\in{{}^{\perp}}(\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)})
by induction on
\lambda
. If
\lambda=\gamma+1
is a successor ordinal, then there is a conflation

\textstyle{C_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\gamma}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C/C_{\gamma},}

where
C_{\gamma}
has a
\gamma
-filtration in
\mbox{Ob}({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J})
and
C/C_{\gamma}\in{{}^{\perp}\mathcal{J}}
. By the induction hypothesis and Lemma
2.1
,
C\in({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J})({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J})^{(\gamma)}=({{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J})^{(\gamma+1)}.

Now we suppose that
\lambda
is a limit ordinal and that the result holds for all ordinals less than
\lambda
. Let
j:J_{0}\to J_{\lambda}
be a morphism in
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
. We need to prove that
{\rm Ext}(C,j):{\rm Ext}(C,J_{0})\to{\rm Ext}(C,J_{\lambda})
is the zero map. Because
j
is a composition of the form
hj_{\lambda}^{0}
, where
j_{\lambda}^{0}:J_{0}\longrightarrow J_{\lambda}
is a
\lambda
-composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
by Proposition
5.4
, we may just assume that
j=j_{\lambda}^{0}
. Note that to prove
{\rm Ext}(C,j)=0
is equivalent to showing that for every conflation
J_{0}\to X\to C
in
{\rm Ext}(C,J_{0}),
the conflation
\Xi_{\lambda}:J_{\lambda}\to X_{\lambda}\to C
that appears in the following pushout diagram is trivial

\textstyle{J_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C.}

By taking the pushout of
J_{0}\to X\to C
along the
(\lambda+1)
-system
(J_{\alpha},j^{\alpha}_{\beta},\alpha<\beta\leq\lambda)
, and taking the pullback along the filtration
0=C_{0}\to C_{1}\to\cdots\to C_{\lambda}
, we obtain the following cubical diagram with all rows conflations.
\bm{J_{0}}
\bm{J_{0}}
\bm{J_{0}}
\bm{J_{1}}
\bm{J_{1}}
\bm{J_{1}}
\bm{J_{\lambda}}
\bm{J_{\lambda}}
\bm{J_{\lambda}}
\bm{X_{0}}
\bm{\bullet}
\bm{X}
\bm{\bullet}
\bm{X_{1}}
\bullet
\bm{\bullet}
\bm{\bullet}
\bm{X_{\lambda}}
\bm{C_{0}}
\bm{C_{1}}
\bm{C_{\lambda}}
\bm{C_{0}}
\bm{C_{1}}
\bm{C_{\lambda}}
\bm{C_{0}}
\bm{C_{1}}
\bm{C_{\lambda}}

Consider the red
(\lambda+1)
-conflation
\mathsf{\Xi}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.36111pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.36111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\sf J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.36111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.36111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\sf X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.0278pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.0278pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\sf C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
appearing in the main diagonal and let us note how the induction hypothesis implies that each of the conflations

\Xi_{\alpha}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.10284pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.10284pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.10284pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.10284pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.87103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.87103pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{\alpha}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

\alpha<\lambda,

is trivial. Indeed, this conflation is obtained from the horizontal pushout at
\alpha,
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{0}_{\alpha}}

Because
j^{0}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}
and
C_{\alpha}
has an
\alpha
-filtration in
{{}^{\perp}}\mathcal{J},

{\rm Ext}(C_{\alpha},j^{0}_{\alpha})=0
and the conflation is trivial.

If we factor the successive structural morphisms of
\mathsf{\Xi}
into their pushout-pullback factorizations, we obtain the system of conflations on the left. Every conflation in that system, except perhaps
\Xi_{\lambda}
in the bottom row, is trivial, because it is either of the form
\Xi_{\alpha},

\alpha<\lambda,
or arises as the pushout/pullback of a trivial conflation.
