The
(\lambda+1)
-conflation on the right, call it
\mathsf{\Sigma},
is obtained from the diagram on the left by composing the pairs that factor through a conflation of the form
\Xi_{\alpha+1}.
Thus
\mathsf{\Sigma}|_{\lambda}
is a
\lambda
-system of trivial conflations. We will show that it satisfies the downward adjustment property. Then Theorem
5.9
applies to show that
\mathsf{\Sigma}|_{\lambda}
is a trivial
\lambda
-conflation. Because the two outside
\lambda
-systems are continuous at
\lambda,
the workhorse Lemma
5.3
will apply to show that
\mathsf{\Sigma}
and therefore
\Sigma_{\lambda}=\Xi_{\lambda}
is trivial, as required.

To verify that splittings adjust downwards for
\mathsf{\Sigma},
there are two cases to consider. When
\gamma=0
or a limit, the conflation of arrows is of the form

\textstyle{\Sigma_{\gamma}\colon}
\textstyle{J_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma_{\gamma+1}\colon}
\textstyle{J_{\gamma+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{\gamma+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{\gamma}}

and will therefore adjust downwards by Corollary
4.6
. For the case when
\gamma=\alpha+1
is a successor, the factorization given in the left diagram satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
4.7
.
∎

6.
Transfinite inductive powers of ghost ideals

In this short section, we generalize Proposition
3.1
to the transfinite situation. To do so, we need the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 6.1
.

Let
\lambda
be an ordinal.
Every continuous
\lambda
-system
{\sf A}
in
\mathcal{A}
whose structural morphisms
a_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}
,
\alpha+1<\lambda
, are inflations in
\mathcal{A}
,
has a composition morphism
A_{0}\longrightarrow\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}A_{\alpha}
that is also an inflation in
\mathcal{A}.

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Hypothesis
6.1
for some infinite ordinal
\lambda
. If
\mathcal{J}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal with
\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J})
a cosyzygy subcategory, then the
\lambda
-th inductive power
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
is still an object-special preenveloping ideal with cosyzygy category
\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))
.

Proof.

Let
A
be an object in the category
\mathcal{A}
. We will construct, by transfinite induction on
\mu\leq\lambda,
a
(\mu+1)
-system
(\Xi_{\alpha},{\xi}_{\beta}^{\alpha}\mid\ \alpha<\beta\leq\mu)
of conflations

\Xi_{\alpha}:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{J_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{\alpha}}

with
j_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\alpha)}
, and
W_{\alpha}\in\alpha
-
\rm Filt(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))
such that for every
\alpha<\mu,
the morphisms
j_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}
and
w_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}
in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Xi_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces:}
\scriptstyle{\xi^{\alpha}_{\beta}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{J_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{W_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w^{\alpha}_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{\alpha+1}:}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\alpha+1}}
\textstyle{J_{\alpha+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{\alpha+1}}

of conflations are inflations.

The inductive step has already been treated in Propostion
3.1
.
For the limit step, assume that for some limit ordinal
\beta\leq\mu
, we have such a
\beta
-system
(\Xi_{\alpha},{\xi}_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\mid\ \alpha<\alpha^{\prime}<\beta)
of conflations in
\mathcal{A}

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.54335pt\raise-115.20828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 42.07161pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 48.07161pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.07161pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 66.17445pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vdots}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 78.2773pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 84.2773pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 90.2773pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 96.2773pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 102.2773pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 116.54335pt\raise-116.95828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\vdots}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

As
{j}_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}
and
{w}_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}
are inflations for every
\alpha<\beta
,
j_{\beta}:=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\beta}}j_{\alpha}
and
{w_{\beta}:=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\beta}}w_{\alpha}}
exist, and

\textstyle{\Xi_{\beta}:=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\beta}}\Xi_{\alpha}:}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{\beta}}

is a conflation in
\mathcal{A}
by Hypothesis
6.1
. By construction and the Ideal Eklof Lemma (Theorem
5.13
),
W_{\beta}\in\beta\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))\subseteq{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{J}^{(\beta)})
. Since
j_{\alpha+1}^{\alpha}\in\mathcal{J}
for every
\alpha<\beta
,
j_{\beta}^{0}=j_{\beta}:A\longrightarrow J_{\beta}
is a
\beta
-transfinite composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{J}
, and therefore,
j_{\beta}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\beta)}
.

Taking
\mu=\lambda
will give us a conflation
\Xi_{\lambda}\colon\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.60501pt\raise 5.99652pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j_{\lambda}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{W_{\lambda}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

such that
j_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
and
W_{\lambda}\in\lambda
-
\rm Filt(\Omega^{-1}(\mathcal{J}))
. Note that
W_{\lambda}
belongs to
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)})
by the Ideal Eklof Lemma, so
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal.
∎

As an immediate consequence, we have:

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has exact coproducts, and satisfies Hypothesis
6.1
for an infinite ordinal
\lambda
. Then the
\lambda
-th inductive power
\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}
is an object-special preenveloping ideal with
\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt({\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S}))
a cosyzygy subcategory. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
has enough projectives, then
(^{\perp}(\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}),\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}})
is a complete ideal cotorsion pair such that
{}^{\perp}(\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}})
is an object ideal with

\operatorname{Ob}(^{\perp}(\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}))=\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{E}\operatorname{-Proj}\star\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt({\rm Sum}(\mathcal{S}))).

Remark 6.4
.

The notions/techniques and results presented so far may be dualized. For a given set
{\mathcal{S}}
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, the ideal left orthogonal to
\langle{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
is called
the ideal of the
{\mathcal{S}}
-coghost morphisms
, and is denoted by
\mathfrak{Cog}_{{\mathcal{S}}}={{}^{\perp}}\langle{\mathcal{S}}\rangle.
On the other hand,
the
projective
\beta
-th power

\mathcal{I}^{\beta}
of an ideal
\mathcal{I}
is defined as the ideal generated by all
\beta
-cocompositions of morphisms in
\mathcal{I}
(dual to Definition
5.1
). Under dual conditions given in Corollary
6.3
, we have
a complete ideal cotorsion pair

(\mathfrak{Cog}_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\beta},\langle\operatorname{add}(\beta\mbox{-}{\rm\mbox{Cofilt}}({\rm{\bf Prod}}(\mathcal{S}))\star\mathcal{E}\operatorname{-Inj})\rangle),

where for a given subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
, the subcategory
\beta\mbox{-}{\rm\mbox{Cofilt}}(\mathcal{C})
denotes the objects having a
\beta\mbox{-}\mathcal{C}
-cofiltration (dual to Definition
5.10
).

7.
The Generalized Generating Hypothesis in Exact Categories

Let
\mathcal{J}
be an ideal. Given ordinals
\mu\geq\lambda,
Proposition
5.4
implies that
\mathcal{J}^{(\mu)}\subseteq\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)},
so there is a decreasing chain of inductive powers of
\mathcal{J}

(3)

\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{J}^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{J}.

In this section, we focus on the question of whether the chain (
3
) stabilizes at some ordinal, that is, if there exists an ordinal
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{J}^{(\mu)}=\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}
for every ordinal
\mu\geq\lambda.
It is hard to answer the question in such a general form, so we consider two special cases of
\mathcal{J}
, each of which comes up naturally in certain contexts of interest.

7.1.
Idempotent ideals

Consider an
idempotent
ideal
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}^{2}.
In that case,
\mathcal{J}^{n}=\mathcal{J}
for every finite ordinal
n\geq 1,
but it is not immediate that the chain (
3
) stabilizes, so it remains to investigate its infinite inductive powers. In order to do so, let us consider some sufficient conditions. Let
\lambda
be a given infinite ordinal.
We say that
\mathcal{J}
is closed under
\lambda
-directed limits
if
\mathcal{J}
has
\lambda
-compositions, and for any continuous
\mu
-system
(J_{\alpha},j_{\beta}^{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\beta<\mu)

in
\mathcal{J}
with
\mu\leq\lambda
and a family of maps
\{g_{\alpha}:J_{\alpha}\to X\mid\alpha<\mu\}
with
g_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{J}
such that
g_{\alpha}=g_{\beta}j^{\alpha}_{\beta}
for
\alpha<\beta<\mu
, the canonical morphism
\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\mu}}g_{\alpha}:\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\mu}}J_{\alpha}\to X
belongs to
\mathcal{J}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be an idempotent ideal in
\mathcal{A}.
If
\mathcal{J}
has
\omega
-compositions, then
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}^{(\omega)}

If
\lambda\geq\omega
is an infinite ordinal, and if
\mathcal{J}
is closed under
\lambda
-directed limits, then
\mathcal{J}^{(\lambda)}=\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\mathcal{J}^{(\omega)}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
is trivial. For the other direction,
let
g:A\longrightarrow J
be a morphism in
\mathcal{J}
. We will construct a continuous
\omega
-system
(J_{n},j_{m}^{n}\mid n<m\in\mathbb{N})
in
\mathcal{J}
with
J_{0}=A
, and a family
\{g_{n}:J_{n}\to J\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
of morphisms with
g_{n}=g_{n+1}j_{n+1}^{n}
such that
g
factors through
{J_{\omega}:=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{n\in\mathbb{N}}}J_{n}}
. Because
J_{0}\longrightarrow J_{\omega}
belongs to
\mathcal{J}^{(\omega)}
, this will show that
g\in\mathcal{J}^{(\omega)}.

Since
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}^{2}
, there exist morphisms
j_{1}^{0}:A\longrightarrow J_{1}
and
g_{1}:J_{1}\longrightarrow J
in
\mathcal{J}
such that
g=g_{0}=g_{1}j_{1}^{0}
. But again, since
g_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{J}
, there exist morphisms
j_{2}^{1}:J_{1}\longrightarrow J_{2}
and
g_{2}:J_{2}\longrightarrow J
in
\mathcal{J}
such that
g_{1}=g_{2}j_{2}^{1}
. Continuing this process, we finally obtain a continuous
\omega
-system of
\mathcal{J}

(J_{n},j_{m}^{n}\mid n<m\in\mathbb{N})
and a family of morphisms
\{g_{n}:J_{n}\to J\mid m\in\mathbb{N}\}
satisfying
g_{n}=g_{n+1}j_{n+1}^{n}
. That
g
factors through
{J_{\omega}:=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{n\in\mathbb{N}}}J_{n}}
follows from the universal property of direct limits.

For the second assertion, if
\mathcal{J}
is closed under
\lambda
-directed limits, then the above construction can keep going on for any ordinal
\alpha<\lambda
, because if
\alpha=\mu
is a limit ordinal, the induced map
g_{\mu}=\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}g_{\alpha}:\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\mu}}J_{\alpha}\to J
belongs to
\mathcal{J}
. Thus we may construct a
\lambda
-system of
\mathcal{J}

(J_{\alpha},j_{\beta}^{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\beta<\lambda)
and a family of maps
\{g_{\alpha}:J_{\alpha}\to J\mid\alpha<\lambda\}
with
g_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{J}
such that
g_{\alpha}=g_{\beta}j^{\alpha}_{\beta}
for
\alpha<\beta<\lambda
, and
g
factors through
\lim\limits_{\longrightarrow\atop{\alpha<\lambda}}J_{\alpha}
follows from the universal property of direct limits, this will establish the result.
∎

7.2.
The Generalized Generating Hypothesis for Ghost ideals

Next we consider the case that
\mathcal{J}
is an
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost ideal
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
, then we have the following decreasing chain of inductive powers of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}

\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{2}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}.

It is clear from the definition of inductive powers of an ideal that any inductive power of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
contains the object ideal

\langle\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\rangle
. Hence, the aforementioned decreasing chain is bounded below by
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\rangle

(4)

\langle\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\rangle\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}^{2}_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}.

We say that
the Generalized
\lambda
-Generating Hypothesis
\lambda
-
GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
holds
if
\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}=\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle.

Remark 7.2
.

The Generating Hypothesis was first introduced by Freyd in
[
16
]
. Lockridge
[
30
]
proposed and studied The Generating Hypothesis for an algebraic triangulated category. Almost at the same time, the study of the Generating Hypothesis for the ghost ideal in the stable module category
kG\operatorname{-\underline{mod}}
(see Example
3.3
) was initiated by Benson, Chebolu, Christensen and Minác̆ in
[
5
]
.
Note that the ghost ideal in
kG\operatorname{-\underline{Mod}}
is trivial if and only if the corresponding ghost ideal in
kG\operatorname{-Mod}
is the object ideal
\langle\operatorname{Proj}\rangle
. In Propositions
8.11
and
8.12
, we show that being trivial for ghost ideals in
\mathbf{K}(R)
and
\mathbf{D}(R)
is equivalent to being an object ideal for the corresponding ghost ideals in the exact categories
\mathbf{C}(R)
and
\operatorname{dg-Proj}
, respectively. From this point of view, the object ideals in an exact category can be considered as ‘trivial’.

Recall that an exact category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
efficient

[
35
]
if it satisfies the following conditions: (i)
\mathcal{A}
is weakly idempotent; (ii) arbitrary transfinite composition of inflations in
\mathcal{A}
exists, and is an inflation, as well; (iii) every object in
\mathcal{A}
is small relative to inflations; (iv)
\mathcal{A}
admits a generator.

Lemma 7.3
.

[
35
, Lemma 1.4 and Theorem 2.13(4)]
and
[
39
, Proposition 5.8]

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is an efficient exact category. Then
\mathcal{A}
has exact coproducts, and for any object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a conflation in
\mathcal{A}
of the form

\Xi:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

with
B\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}
and
C\in\rm Filt({\mathcal{S}})
, that is, the cotorsion pair
(^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}),{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})
has enough injectives.

The above lemma can be used to prove the following result.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is an efficient exact category. Then
\lambda
-
GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
holds if and only if
for every ordinal
\mu\geq\lambda
,

\mathfrak{g}^{(\mu)}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}.

Proof.

If
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
holds, then it is clear that for every ordinal
\mu\geq\lambda
,

\mathfrak{g}^{(\mu)}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}.
Conversely, let
f:A\to X
be a morphism in
\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}
. By the above lemma, there is a conflation

\Xi:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.83679pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

with
B\in{{\mathcal{S}}}^{\perp}
and
C\in\mu\mbox{-}\rm Filt({\mathcal{S}})
for some
\mu
, and hence
C\in\mu\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}}))
. If
\mu\leq\lambda
, then
C\in\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}}))
, and hence
{\rm Ext}(C,f)=0
by The Ideal Eklof Lemma (Theorem
5.13
). If
\mu\geq\lambda
, then
{\rm Ext}(C,f)=0
follows from
f\in\mathfrak{g}^{(\mu)}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{g}^{(\lambda)}_{\mathcal{S}}
. Therefore
f
always factors through
B
, and so
f\in\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle
.
∎

Now we handle the question of whether there exists an ordinal
\lambda
such that
\lambda
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
holds. Recall that a cardinal number
\lambda
is just the least ordinal of cardinality
\lambda
.
So far,
\lambda
has been considered as an arbitrary ordinal. Now we switch
\lambda
to a cardinal number, which can be still considered as an ordinal when needed to work with inductive powers of an ideal and filtrations.

Recall that an object
A
in
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
\lambda
-presentable
if the functor
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(A,-)

preserves
\lambda
-directed colimits. The category
\mathcal{A}
is called

locally
\lambda
-presentable
if it is cocomplete and there is a
set
\mathcal{C}
of
\lambda
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{A}
such that any other object in
\mathcal{A}
is a
\lambda
-directed colimit of objects in

\mathcal{C}
. In case
\lambda=\omega
, a
\lambda
-presentable object is called
finitely presented
and a locally
\lambda
-presentable category is known as
locally finitely presentable
. A category
\mathcal{A}
is called
locally presentable
if it is locally
\lambda
-presentable for some cardinal
\lambda
. Every object in a locally presentable category is
\lambda^{\prime}
-presentable for some regular cardinal
\lambda^{\prime}
; see
[
1
, Proposition 1.16]
. For a detailed treatment on locally presentable categories, see
[
1
]
.

It is well known that if
\mathcal{A}
is a Grothendieck category with the absolute exact structure, then
\mathcal{A}
is locally
\lambda
-presentable for some infinite regular cardinal
\lambda
; see
[
3
, Proposition 3.10]
. For a given class
{\mathcal{S}}
of
\lambda
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{A}
, Št́ovíček in
[
38
, Theorem 3.1]
showed that an object
X
which has an
{\mathcal{S}}
-filtration, has a
\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
-filtration of length
\leq\lambda
. This gives a global bound on the length of the
\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
-filtration for objects in
\rm Filt({\mathcal{S}})
. Enochs obtained a similar bound in the settting of the category of modules (see
[
14
]
), so we call it the
Enochs-Št́ovíček bound
on filtrations for objects in
\rm Filt
-
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Suppose that
\lambda
is an infinite regular cardinal, and
\mathcal{A}
is a locally
\lambda
-presentable Grothendieck category. If
\mathcal{S}
is a set of
\lambda
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{A}
such that
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp})
contains a generating set for
\mathcal{A}
, then the Generalized
\lambda
-Generating Hypothesis holds for
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

From
[
20
, Lemma 3.6]
, we have

{}^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})={\rm add}(\mbox{Filt}({\mathcal{S}})),

which also implies that
(^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}),{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})
is a complete cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
.
On the other hand, the Enochs-Št́ovíček bound tells us that

\mbox{Filt}({\mathcal{S}})=\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}})),
(here
\lambda
is thought as an ordinal in the usual way). Then

\langle^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})\rangle=\langle\mbox{Filt}({\mathcal{S}})\rangle=\langle\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}}))\rangle\ .

By Theorem
6.3
,
\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}}))
is a cosyzygy subcategory of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(\lambda)}
, so

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(\lambda)}\subseteq\langle\lambda\mbox{-}\rm Filt(\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}}))\rangle^{\perp}=\langle^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})\rangle^{\perp}

Note that
(\langle^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})\rangle,\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle)
is an ideal cotorsion pair in
\mathcal{A}
by
[
17
, Theorem 28]
. Therefore

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}^{(\lambda)}\subseteq\langle^{\perp}({\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})\rangle^{\perp}=\langle{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}\rangle.

∎

By the considerations above, every object of a Grothendieck category
\mathcal{A}
is presentable, so if
{\mathcal{S}}
is a set of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, there is an infinite cardinal
\lambda
such that
\mathcal{A}
is locally
\lambda
-presentable and
{\mathcal{S}}
is a set of
\lambda
-presentable objects in
\mathcal{A}.

Remark 7.6
.

Let us look more closely at the case when
\lambda
(regarded as an ordinal) is
\omega,
as it will be the main source of applications in the next sections. Theorem
7.5
ensures that if
\mathcal{A}
is a locally finitely presentable Grothendieck category, and
\mathcal{S}
is a set of finitely presented objects in
\mathcal{A}
such that
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp})
contains a generating set for
\mathcal{A}
(for instance, if
\mathcal{A}
has enough projective objects), then
\omega
-GGH(
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}})
holds, or equivalently, that the
\omega
-inductive power
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}^{{}(\omega)}
of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the ideal
\langle\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\rangle
.

Remark 7.7
.

By contrast to Remark
6.4
, we are not able to dualize the results of this section. This is due to the absence of a theory of cofiltrations and their bounds.

8.
The ghost ideal in
\mathbf{K}(R)
,
\mathbf{D}(R)
and
\mathbf{C}(R)

From now on,
R
denotes an associative ring with identity. Let
R\mbox{-Mod}
be the category of left
R
-modules,
\mathbf{C}(R)
the category of chain complexes of left
R
-modules,
\mathbf{K}(R)
the homotopy category of chain complexes of left
R
-modules, and
\mathbf{D}(R)
the derived category of chain complexes of left
R
-modules.

In this section, we investigate ghost morphisms and ghost ideals in the category
\mathbf{C}(R)
, and its relation with the existent ones in
\mathbf{D}(R)
and
\mathbf{K}(R)
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
A\in R
-
Mod
,
the
n
th sphere chain complex

\operatorname{S}^{n}(A)
is the chain complex of the form

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

with
A
in the
n
th place;
the
n
th disc chain complex

\operatorname{D}^{n}(A)
is the chain complex of the form

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{A}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

with two
A
’s in the
n
-th and
(n-1)
-th places.

The
translation functor on
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
is the autofunctor
\Sigma:\ \mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
defined by

\Sigma(X)_{n}:=X_{n-1}

and with differential
d^{\Sigma(X)}=-d^{X}
. Its inverse is denoted by
\Sigma^{-1}
. For a given morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
, the
cone
of
f
, denoted by
\operatorname{cone}(f)
, is a chain complex defined by
\operatorname{cone}(f)_{n}:=Y_{n}\oplus X_{n-1}
with differentials

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}d_{n}^{Y}&f_{n-1}\\
0&-d_{n-1}^{X}\end{array}\right]:Y_{n}\oplus X_{n-1}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\oplus X_{n-2}.

The cone of
f
induces the following canonical exact sequence in
\mathbf{C}(\mathcal{A})
defined by degree-wise inclusion and projection

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.01389pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.01389pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.01389pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.01389pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{cone}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.9862pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 94.9862pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

8.1.
The Ghost ideal in
\mathbf{C}(R)
and the Cartan-Eilenberg exact structure

Note that for any integer
n
, there are four basic functors from the category
\mathbf{C}(R)
to
R\mbox{-Mod}
: the
n
th cycle functor
\operatorname{Z}_{n}
, the
n
th cokernel functor
\mbox{C}_{n}
, the
n
th boundary functor
\operatorname{B}_{n}
, and the
n
th homology functor
\operatorname{H}_{n}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

[
13
, Lemma 5.2]
Let
\Xi:\ 0\rightarrow X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z\rightarrow 0
be a short exact sequence of chain complexes of left
R
-modules. The following are equivalent:

(i)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sequence
\operatorname{Z}_{n}(\Xi)
is exact.

(ii)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sequence
\operatorname{B}_{n}(\Xi)
is exact.

(iii)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sequence
\operatorname{H}_{n}(\Xi)
is exact.

(iv)

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sequence
\operatorname{C}_{n}(\Xi)
is exact.

Definition 8.2
.

A short exact sequence
\Xi:0\rightarrow X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z\rightarrow 0
of chain complexes of left
R
-modules is said to be
Cartan-Eilenberg exact

(
\operatorname{CE}
-exact
for short
)
if one of the equivalent conditions given in Proposition
8.1
is satisfied.

It is easy to verify that the class
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}
consisting of Cartan-Eilenberg short exact sequences in
\mathbf{C}(R)
is an exact structure on
\mathbf{C}(R)
, we call it the
Cartan-Eilenberg exact structure
on
\mathbf{C}(R)
.

Next, we always let
E
be an injective cogenerator in the category
R\mbox{-Mod}
of left
R
-modules. We have the following result.

Proposition 8.3
.

The Cartan-Eilenberg exact structure
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}
is projectively generated by the set
\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and is injectively generated by the set
\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(E)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

The first statement follows from Proposition
8.1
(i) and the fact

{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(\operatorname{S}^{n}(R),\Xi)\cong{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(R,\operatorname{Z}_{n}(\Xi))\cong\operatorname{Z}_{n}(\Xi).

For the second one, we have

{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(\Xi,\operatorname{S}^{n}(E))\cong{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(\operatorname{C}_{n}(\Xi),E).

Since
E
an injective cogenerator in
R\mbox{-}{\rm Mod}
,
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(\operatorname{C}_{n}(\Xi),E)
is a short exact sequence of abelian groups if and only if
\operatorname{C}_{n}(\Xi)
is a short exact sequence of left
R
-modules. Applying Proposition
8.1
(iv), we have the second statement.
∎

Note that the set
\{\operatorname{D}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a generating set for
\mathbf{C}(R)
with projective chain complexes, and the exact structure
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}
is clearly also projectively generated by
\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(R),\operatorname{D}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Dually, the exact structure
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}
is injectively generated by the set
\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(E),\operatorname{D}^{n}(E)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, which contains a cogenerating set
\{\operatorname{D}^{n}(E)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore the exact category
(\mathbf{C}(R);\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}})
has enough projectives and injectives by Proposition
2.2
.

Proposition 8.4
.

[
13
, Propositions 3.3 and 3.4.]

Let
X\in\mathbf{C}(R)
. Then
X
is a projective object in
(\mathbf{C}(R);\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}})
, called
\operatorname{CE}
-projective, if and only if for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
\operatorname{Z}_{n}(X)
,
\operatorname{B}_{n}(X)
,
\operatorname{H}_{n}(X)
and
\operatorname{C}_{n}(X)
are projective left
R
-modules if and only if it is isomorphic to a chain complex of the form

\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{D}^{n}(P_{n})\oplus\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{S}^{n}(Q_{n})

where
P_{n}
and
Q_{n}
are projective left
R
-modules, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Similarly,
X
is an injective object in
(\mathbf{C}(R);\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}})
, called
\operatorname{CE}
-injective, if and only if for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
\operatorname{Z}_{n}(X)
,
\operatorname{B}_{n}(X)
,
\operatorname{H}_{n}(X)
and
\operatorname{C}_{n}(X)
are injective left
R
-modules if and only if it is isomorphic to a chain complex of the form

\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{D}^{n}(I_{n})\oplus\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{S}^{n}(E_{n})

where all
I_{n}
and
E_{n}
are injective left
R
-modules.

We let
\operatorname{CE-Proj}
and
\operatorname{CE-Inj}
be the classes of CE-projective and CE-injective chain complexes of left
R
-modules, respectively. Then
\operatorname{CE-Proj}=\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}\operatorname{-Proj}
and
\operatorname{CE-Inj}=\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}\operatorname{-Inj}
.

Definition 8.5
.

A morphism
f
in
\mathbf{C}(R)
is called a
ghos
t map if
\operatorname{H}_{n}(f)=0
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We denote by
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
the class of all ghost morphisms in
\mathbf{C}(R)
which is clearly an ideal. Recall from Remark
6.4
that if
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is a subset, the ideal left orthogonal to
\langle{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
consists of the
{\mathcal{S}}
-coghost morphisms and is denoted by
\mathfrak{Cog}_{{\mathcal{S}}}={{}^{\perp}}\langle{\mathcal{S}}\rangle
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{S}:=\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(E)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
\mathbf{C}(R)
. We have

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))=\mathfrak{Cog}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Let
f
be a morphism in
\mathbf{C}(R)
. Since
\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)
is a degree-wise projective chain complex, we have

{\rm Ext}(\operatorname{S}^{n}(R),f)={\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{K}(R)}(\operatorname{S}^{n-1}(R),f).

On the other hand, one can easily show that

{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{K}(R)}(\operatorname{S}^{n-1}(R),f)\cong\operatorname{H}_{n-1}(f).

Therefore,
f
is
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost if and only if
f
is ghost as given in Definition
8.5
.

As for the second equality, since
\operatorname{S}^{n}(E)
is a degree-wise injective chain complex,

{\rm Ext}(f,\operatorname{S}^{n}(R))={\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{K}(R)}(f,\operatorname{S}^{n+1}(E)).

Again, we have

{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{K}(R)}(f,\operatorname{S}^{n+1}(E))\cong{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}(\operatorname{H}_{n+1}(f),E).

Since
E
is a cogenerator, we have that
f
is
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
-coghost if and only if
f
is ghost in
\mathbf{C}(R)
.
∎

The following is an immediate consequence of Proposition
8.6

Corollary 8.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathbf{C}(R)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

f
is a ghost map in
\mathbf{C}(R)
;

(ii)

f
is
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}
-phantom;

(iii)

f
is
\mathcal{E}_{\operatorname{CE}}
-cophantom;

(iv)

The pullback of the short exact sequence
0\to\Sigma^{-1}(Y)\to\operatorname{cone}(1_{\Sigma^{-1}(Y)})\to Y\to 0
along
f
is
\operatorname{CE}
-exact;

(v)

The pushout of the short exact sequence
0\to X\to\operatorname{cone}(1_{X})\to\Sigma X\to 0
along
f
is
\operatorname{CE}
-exact.

Proof.

The equivalence of the statements (i), (ii) and (iii) and the implications (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) and (iii)
\Rightarrow
(v) follow from Propositions
8.3
and
8.6
.
For (iv)
\Rightarrow
(i), consider the pullback diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{cone}(1_{\Sigma^{-1}(Y)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Since
0\to\Sigma^{-1}(Y)\to H\to X\to 0
is CE-exact, for any integer
n
, every map
g:\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)\to X
factors through
H
, and hence the composition
fg
factors through
\operatorname{cone}(1_{\Sigma^{-1}(Y)})
. This shows that
fg
is null homotopic, and so
\operatorname{H}_{n}(f)=0
.

A similar argument can be applied to prove the implication (v)
\Rightarrow
(i).
∎

In the following, we show that
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
is in fact in the middle of a complete ideal cotorsion triple whose left and right perpendicular ideals of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
have simpler forms.

Proposition 8.8
.

There exists a complete ideal cotorsion triple

(\langle\operatorname{CE-Proj}\rangle,\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)),\langle\operatorname{CE-Inj}\rangle).

Proof.

The inclusion
\langle\mbox{CE-Proj}\rangle\subseteq{}^{\perp}(\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)))
follows from Proposition
8.6
immediately.
Conversely, by Proposition
3.7
, it is enough to show the inclusion
\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\star\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}})\subseteq\mbox{CE-Proj}
. If
X\in\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\star\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
, then there exists a short exact sequence

(5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
P\in\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))
and
T\in\mbox{Sum}({\mathcal{S}})
. Since
P
is an acyclic chain complex, it is easy to verify that the short exact sequence given in (
5
) is CE-exact. Note that, all cycles, boundaries, homologies and cokernels of both
P
and
T
are projectives. Therefore,
X
is CE-projective, as well.

The other part of the ideal cotorsion triple comes from Corollary
8.7
, and the dual argument of above. ∎

For the
n
-th power of the ghost ideal,
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{n}
, we have the following result by Corollary
3.8
.

Proposition 8.9
.

For any
n\geq 1
, there exists a complete ideal cotorsion triple

(\langle\operatorname{add}(n\operatorname{-Filt(CE-Proj)})\rangle,\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{n},\langle\operatorname{add}(n\operatorname{-Cofilt(CE-Inj)})\rangle).

Notice that in the previous proposition, we have used the fact that finite projective and inductive powers of an ideal are the same. However, as the infinite inductive and projective powers of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
may not coincide,
apparently there is no reason for
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{(\lambda)}
to be a part of a cotorsion triple of ideals of the form just as given in the previous result.

8.2.
The Relation between ghost ideals in
\mathbf{C}(R)
,
\mathbf{D}(R)
and
\mathbf{K}(R)

Note that there exist the canonical functors

(6)

\textstyle{\mathbf{C}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}(R),}

which are the identity on objects. The first one is the canonical quotient functor, and the second one is the localization functor. For a given morphism
f\in\mathbf{C}(R)
, we denote its image in
\mathbf{K}(R)
by
\overline{f}
.

For any integer
n
, the
n
th homology functor
\operatorname{H}_{n}:\ \mathbf{C}(R)\longrightarrow R\mbox{-}{\rm Mod}
is factorized over
\mathbf{K}(R)
and
\mathbf{D}(R)

(7)

\operatorname{H}_{n}:\ \mathbf{C}(R)\longrightarrow\mathbf{K}(R)\longrightarrow\mathbf{D}(R)\longrightarrow R\mbox{-}{\rm Mod}.

A morphism
\overline{f}
in
\mathbf{K}(R)
is called
ghost
if it induces zero on homologies, that is,
\operatorname{H}_{n}(\overline{f})=0
for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Ghost morphisms in
\mathbf{K}(R)
form an ideal, which will be denoted by
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))
.
On the other hand, Christensen
[
11
, Section 8]
calls
ghost
a morphism
g
in
\mathbf{D}(R)
if
\operatorname{H}_{n}(g)=0
, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. They also form an ideal in
\mathbf{D}(R)
, which will be denoted by
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))
.

Using the factorization of the homology functor given in (
7
), it is immediate that the composition (
6
) of functors induces the following natural transformations between finite powers of ghost ideals

\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))^{n}.}

The quotient functor
\mathbf{C}(R)\longrightarrow\mathbf{K}(R)
is full, and therefore, the induced natural transformation

(8)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))^{n}}

is in fact an epimorphism.

We denote the class of all acyclic chain complexes of left
R
-modules by
Acyc
.
Recall that a chain complex
X
of projective left
R
-modules is said to be
dg
-
projective
if for every acyclic chain complex
Y
of left
R
-modules,
{\rm\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbf{K}(R)}(X,Y)=0
. Let
\operatorname{dg-Proj}
denote the class of all
dg
-projective chain complexes in
\mathbf{C}(R)
. The crucial fact which will be used in sequel is that the pair
(\operatorname{dg-Proj},\mbox{Acyc})
is a complete cotorsion pair in
\mathbf{C}(R)
. Dually, the class
dg-Inj
of dg-injective chain complex of left
R
-modules is defined, and we have a complete cotorsion pair
(\operatorname{Acyc},\mbox{dg-Inj})
in
\mathbf{C}(R)
; see
[
15
, Theorem 4.1.4, Example 4.1.8, Corollary 4.1.15]
.

It is well known that there exists a projective model structure on
\mathbf{C}(R)
whose homotopy category is equivalent to
\mathbf{D}(R)
. In fact, this projective model structure on
\mathbf{C}(R)
is induced from the triple
(\operatorname{dg-Proj},\operatorname{Acyc},\mathbf{C}(R))
, see
[
24
, Example 3.3]
. This projective model structure implies that the composition of canonical functors

\theta:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 28.00697pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-28.00697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.00697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.00697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 52.00697pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{K}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.46877pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 106.46877pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{D}(R)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

is an equivalence of categories, where
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
denotes the quotient category of
dg-Proj
modulo homotopic to zero maps.
Note that the inverse of the equivalence
\theta:\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})\longrightarrow\mathbf{D}(R)
assigns to every chain complex
X
of left
R
-modules a dg-projective cofibrant replacement
P_{X}
of
X
: there exists a quasi-isomorphism
\sigma_{X}:P_{X}\longrightarrow X
in
\mathbf{C}(R)
, where
P_{X}
is a dg-projective chain complex. To every morphism
g:X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{D}(R)
,
\theta^{-1}
assigns a morphism
\overline{g^{\prime}}:P_{X}\longrightarrow P_{Y}
in
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
such that
\theta^{-1}(g)=\overline{g^{\prime}}
in such a way that the following diagram commutes in
\mathbf{D}(R)

\textstyle{P_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{P_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y}

As an aside, the morphisms
\sigma_{X}
and
\sigma_{Y}
are isomorphisms in
\mathbf{D}(R)
.

Note that
dg-Proj
is an extension closed subcategory of
\mathbf{C}(R)
, and therefore, is an exact subcategory of
\mathbf{C}(R)
. So we have
the following commutative diagram

(9)

dg-Proj
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\mathbf{C}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{K}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}(R),}

We let
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj}))
and
\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
denote the classes of ghost morphisms in
\mathbf{K}(R)
and
\mathbf{C}(R)
, respectively, between dg-projective chain complexes. It is immediate that both classes
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj}))
and
\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
are ideals in
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
and
dg-Proj
, respectively.

Clearly, if
\overline{f}
is a ghost morphism in
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj}))
, then its image
\theta(\overline{f})
in
\mathbf{D}(R)
is also a ghost morphism. Conversely, if
g:X\longrightarrow Y
is a ghost morphism in
\mathbf{D}(R)
, then there exists a unique morphism
\overline{g^{\prime}}:P_{X}\longrightarrow P_{Y}
in
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
such that
\sigma_{Y}\overline{g^{\prime}}=g\sigma_{X}
in
\mathbf{D}(R)
. Applying the homology functor
\operatorname{H}_{*}
to the composition, we see that
\overline{g^{\prime}}
is a ghost morphism in
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
. Therefore, the equivalence
\theta
induces the isomorphism
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj}))\cong\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))
of ideals.

Using the commutative diagram given in (
9
) and the composition (
7
), we have that if
f
is a morphism in
\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
, then its image in
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})\cong\mathbf{D}(R)
is ghost.
Therefore, the diagram (
9
) induces the following commutative diagram of ghost ideals

(10)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))}

Note that the
n
th sphere chain complex
\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)
is a dg-projective chain complex, so
{\mathcal{S}}:=\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is also a set of objects in the exact category
dg-Proj
. Therefore in the exact category
\operatorname{dg-Proj}
, the
{\mathcal{S}}
-ghost ideal

\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
is just the ideal
\mathfrak{g}(\operatorname{dg-Proj}).

8.3.
The Generalized Generating Hypothesis

As already mentioned, the following triple is a complete cotorsion triple in
\mathbf{C}(R)

(\mbox{dg-Proj},\mbox{Acyc},\mbox{dg-Inj}).

By
[
17
, Theorem 28]
, it yields a complete ideal cotorsion triple in
\mathbf{C}(R)

(\langle\mbox{dg-Proj}\rangle,\langle\mbox{Acyc}\rangle,\langle\mbox{dg-Inj}\rangle).

On the other hand, it is well known that

{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}=\mbox{Acyc}={}^{\perp}{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},

where

{\mathcal{S}}:=\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:=\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(E)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
Therefore, the chain for the inductive powers of
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
given in (
4
) is bounded below by the object ideal
\langle\mbox{Acyc}\rangle
, that is,

\langle\mbox{Acyc}\rangle\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{(\lambda)}\subseteq\cdots\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))^{2}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R)).

Since
\mathbf{C}(R)
is a locally finitely presentable Grothendieck category, Theorem
7.5
gives the following result.

Proposition 8.10
.

\omega
-
GGH(
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rm{)}
always holds.

The next proposition relates the Generalized
1
-Generating Hypothesis for
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
with the triviality of the ideal
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))
.

Proposition 8.11
.

1
-
GGH(
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rm{)}
holds if and only if
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))=0
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))=0
. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
. Using the diagram (
10
), its image
\overline{f}
in
\mathbf{K}(R)
belongs to
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(R))
. By assumption,
\overline{f}
is homotopic to zero. From
[
8
, Remark 9.11]
, we know that
f
factors through the cone of
1_{X}
, which is contractible. So
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))\subseteq\langle\mbox{Acyc}\rangle
.

Conversely, if
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))=\langle\mbox{Acyc}\rangle
, then

\langle\mbox{dg-Proj}\rangle={}^{\perp}\langle\mbox{Acyc}\rangle={}^{\perp}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))=\langle\mbox{CE-Proj}\rangle.
It implies that a projective resolution of any left
R
-module is CE-projective, and hence, every left
R
-module is projective. So every acyclic chain complex of left
R
-modules is contractible. As indicated before, if
\overline{f}
is a ghost morphism in
\mathbf{K}(R)
, then
f
is ghost in
\mathbf{C}(R)
. But it is factorized over a contractible chain complex, so
\overline{f}=0
.
∎

Note that
dg-Proj
is a Frobenius exact category with a complete cotorsion triple

(\mbox{dg-Proj},\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R)),\mbox{dg-Proj}),

where
\operatorname{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))
is the class of projective objects in
\mathbf{C}(R)
which consists of contractible chain complexes of projective left
R
-modules.
Again, by using
[
17
, Theorem 28]
,

(\langle\mbox{dg-Proj}\rangle,\langle\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rangle,\langle\mbox{dg-Proj}\rangle)

is a complete ideal cotorsion triple in
dg-Proj
. Similarly,

{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp}=\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))
in
dg-Proj
, where
{\mathcal{S}}:=\{\operatorname{S}^{n}(R)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore, the chain (
4
) for
\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
is bounded below by
\langle\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rangle

\langle\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))\rangle\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})^{(\lambda)}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})^{2}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj}).

Proposition 8.12
.

For any integer
n\geq 1
,
n
-
GGH(
\mathfrak{g}(\operatorname{dg-Proj})\rm{)}
holds if and only if
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))^{n}=0

Proof.

We only prove the statement for
n=1
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))=0
. Let
f
be a map in
{\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})}
. From the diagram given in (
10
), the image
\overline{f}
of
f
in
\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
belongs to
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{K}(\mbox{dg-Proj}))\cong\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))=0
, and therefore,
\overline{f}
is homotopic to zero. From
[
8
, Remark 9.11]
,
f
factors through the cone of the domain of
f
which is acyclic and dg-projective, and therefore, is a projective chain complex.
The other side follows from the diagram (
10
) and the fact that the intersection
\mbox{dg-Proj}\cap\mbox{Acyc}
is exactly the class
\mbox{Proj}(\mathbf{C}(R))
.
∎

We should point out that for a positive integer
n
, the Generalized
n
-Generating Hypothesis for both ideals
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{C}(R))
and
\mathfrak{g}(\mbox{dg-Proj})
forces to impose certain conditions on the ring
R
, namely, finiteness of the left global dimension of
R
. In fact, Hovey and Lockridge showed in
[
26
, Lemma 1.4]
that if
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))^{n+1}=0
, then the left global dimension of
R
, denoted by
\operatorname{l.gl.dim}(R)
, is
\leq n
. On the other hand, by
[
11
, Theorem 8.3]
, we know that if
\operatorname{l.gl.dim}(R)\leq n
, then
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbf{D}(R))^{n+1}=0
. Now, we are able to obtain these results in our setting by using tools from ideal approximation theory. For this end, we need a filtration version of the statement
[
11
, Theorem 8.3]
.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
X
be a dg-projective chain complex of left
R
-modules. If for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the projective dimensions of
\operatorname{H}_{i}(X)
and
\operatorname{Z}_{i}(X)
are
\leq n
,
then
X
is a direct summand of an object in

(n+1)\operatorname{-Filt}(\operatorname{CE-Proj})
.

Proof.

We will apply induction on
n
. Let
n=0
. Suppose that
X
is a dg-projective chain complex of left
R
-modules with projective homologies and cycles. For every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, consider the induced short exact sequences

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{B}_{i}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.75986pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.75986pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Z}_{i}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 143.04749pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 143.04749pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{H}_{i}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 200.724pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 200.724pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
